NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm6 sjogren syndrome</b>	<b>Name:</b> Mr. James Bridge (referred to as "Mr. Britt" in the case) - <b>Age:</b> 66 years old - <b>Medical History:</b> Sjögren's Syndrome (diagnosed 19 months ago) - <b>Chief Dental Findings:</b> Xerostomia + Multiple Root Carious Lesions	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_sjogren_syndrome NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm6 sjogren syndrome</b> — list the key points for: <i>D. Prescription Sialogogues (If Non-Pharmacological Fails)</i>	<ul><li>Refer to physician for prescription salivary-inducing medications</li><li>Recommended agents: <b>Pilocarpine</b> or <b>Cevimeline</b> (cholinergic agonists)</li><li>Dentist does <b>not</b> prescribe these — but should <b>recommend a physician consult</b> if OTC strategies are insufficient</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_sjogren_syndrome NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 sjogren syndrome</b> — list the key points for: <i>C. <b>Mmm6 sjogren syndrome</b> — list the key points for: <i>E. <b>Mmm6 sjogren syndrome</b> — give details for Priority: <i><b>1st choice</b></i>	<b>Material:</b> <b>RMGIC</b> (Resin-Modified Glass Ionomer Cement)<br><b>When to Use:</b> When adequate moisture control is achievable; evidence-based, better prognosis	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_sjogren_syndrome NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 sjogren syndrome</b> — give details for Problem: <i>Xerostomia</i>	<b>Preventive Measure:</b> Frequent water sips, humidifier<br><b>Key Detail:</b> Lifestyle modification	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_sjogren_syndrome NDECC_SJ
NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm6 phone call patient pain</b>	A regular patient calls you by phone from a different part of the same province where you both live and practice. You are still their dentist; they still fall under your care. The call is a regular phone call — <b>not</b> an encrypted virtual platform. The core challenge: you cannot diagnose or treat over a phone call, but you still have a professional duty to respond appropriately.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_phone_call_patient_pain NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm6 phone call patient pain</b> — for each <i>Scenario</i>, give the corresponding <i>Action</i>.	<ul><li><b>Mild pain, no red flags</b> → OTC analgesic + schedule clinic visit next day</li><li><b>Moderate pain, known cavity</b> → Schedule earliest appointment + OTC analgesic bridge</li><li><b>Severe pain / red flags (fever, swelling, difficulty breathing)</b> → Refer immediately to nearest dentist for emergency care</li><li><b>Patient wants remote diagnosis</b> → Use RCDSO-approved encrypted platform only — but patient still needs in-person visit</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_phone_call_patient_pain NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm6 cra erosion kid</b> — list the key points for: <i>Critical Flag: Age of the Child</i>	<ul><li><b>Always begin by asking:</b> <i>"Could you please remind me of the child's name, gender, and age?"</i></li><li>A smart, natural phrasing: <i>"Could you please remind me of the child's age again?"</i> (sounds like a chart confirmation)</li><li>The simulator in this station reveals: <b>the child's birthday is this Sunday — she is turning 7 years old; currently ~6 years, 11 months</b></li><li>Once a preferred pronoun (e.g., <i>she/her</i>) is given, <b>use it consistently</b> for the rest of the station — switching pronouns is a critical error</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_cra_erosion_kid NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 cra erosion kid</b> — list the key points for: <i>M1 — Medical History</i>	<ul><li>Ask: <i>"Does your grandchild have any medical conditions?"</i></li><li><b>Station answer:</b> No known medical history</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_cra_erosion_kid NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 cra erosion kid</b> — list the key points for: <i>M2 — Medications</i>	<ul><li>Ask: <i>"Is your grandchild taking any medications?"</i></li><li><b>Station answer:</b> Yes — multivitamin <b>gummies</b></li><li><b>Always ask the form</b> (tablet vs. gummy) — gummy vitamins are sticky, cariogenic, lodge in pits and fissures; they are a significant caries risk factor</li><li>Note: switching to <b>tablet form</b> is a targeted preventive recommendation</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_cra_erosion_kid NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 cra erosion kid</b> — list the key points for: <i>P1 — Practices</i>	<ul><li>Explain: <i>regular, consistent dental visits actually save money long-term</i> through early detection and intervention, rather than costly bulk treatment later</li><li>Address the financial barrier directly and compassionately</li><li>Link to clinical finding: <i>"I found evidence of enamel erosion on your grandchild's teeth, which is consistent with regular soda consumption"</i></li><li>Replace with <b>water</b> between meals; <b>milk</b> only with meals (breakfast, lunch, dinner)</li><li>Child is not yet capable of independent brushing (cannot tie shoelaces = insufficient dexterity)</li><li>Grandparent should <b>assist or at minimum supervise</b> brushing and flossing</li><li>Flossing may require <b>parental/caregiver assistance up to age 10</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_cra_erosion_kid NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 cra erosion kid</b> — list the key points for: <i>P2 — Products (Financially Sensitive)</i>	<ul><li>Family is using "natural/chemical-free" toothpaste — likely <b>non-fluoridated</b></li><li>Recommend switching to <b>OTC fluoridated toothpaste</b> — check label for fluoride content</li><li>For a child ≥6 years: up to <b>1,000–1,500 ppm fluoride</b> is appropriate</li><li><b>Pea-sized amount</b> (not smear — smear is for children under 3 years)</li><li>Clinically appropriate for high caries risk — but <b>very expensive (~$45–50 vs. $4–5 for OTC)</b></li><li>Do <b>not</b> prescribe outright to a financially limited family</li><li>Recommended approach: <i>"Let's start with OTC fluoridated toothpaste. If needed, I can write a prescription for a stronger toothpaste down the road"</i></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_cra_erosion_kid NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 cra erosion kid</b> — give details for Detail: <i>Brushing frequency</i>	<b>Station Answer:</b> 2× daily<br><b>Flag:</b> Acceptable	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_cra_erosion_kid NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm6 tx plan phase</b>	A 46‑year‑old patient presents with pain in the lower right posterior region (46) due to infected root stumps, with multiple carious and broken‑down teeth, several missing teeth, and significant financial limitations. OPG is available but further periapicals/bitewings are required for definitive diagnosis and restorability assessment.  (You can say this in 1 short paragraph if the station requires a “brief summary”.)	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_tx_plan_phase NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 tx plan phase</b> — list the step-by-step approach / framework (in order).	<ol><li>Phase 0 – Emergency / pain management and finances</li><li>Phase 1 – Disease control</li><li>Phase 2 – Rehabilitation / prosthetic rehabilitation</li><li>Phase 3 – Maintenance and recall</li></ol>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_tx_plan_phase NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 tx plan phase</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 0 – Emergency and financial planning</i>	<ul><li>Extract the infected, symptomatic root stumps at 46 (4‑6) as an emergency procedure.</li><li>Mention you will not delay this even if the patient cannot pay immediately; pain relief is obligatory emergency care.</li><li>Take necessary radiographs for diagnosis and treatment planning</li><li>Bitewings for caries extent and bone levels in posterior segments.</li><li>Periapicals for teeth with suspected periapical pathology, previous endodontics, or where restorability is uncertain.</li><li>Explain the full phased treatment plan and then immediately discuss financial alternatives, because she has “very limited finances”.</li><li>Options to mention</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_tx_plan_phase NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 tx plan phase</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 1 – Disease control</i>	<ul><li>Teeth broken down to or near gingival level, with poor crown–root ratio or poor restorability on OPG and clinical exam. (In your notes you might list: e.g., 26, 27, 37, etc., but in a generic station, you can say “extrac…</li><li>Write: “Extract unrestorable teeth with hopeless prognosis (e.g., 26, 27 – correlate clinically and radiographically).”</li><li>Restore all restorable carious lesions identified on the OPG and confirmed with bitewings</li><li>Primary caries: e.g., 16, 15, 14, 35, 34, 33, etc. – write generically as “restoration of all cavitated carious lesions using suitable restorative materials.”</li><li>Recurrent caries around existing restorations and previous endodontically treated teeth: e.g., 12, 21 – “re‑evaluate and replace defective restorations with recurrent caries.”</li><li>For each area you can add: “Bitewing radiographs recommended to assess depth and proximity to pulp.”</li><li>For teeth with</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_tx_plan_phase NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 tx plan phase</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 2 – Rehabilitation / prosthetic rehabilitation</i>	<ul><li>Start Phase 2 only when</li><li>Caries and active periodontal disease are controlled.</li><li>Perio re‑eval is satisfactory.</li><li>If there is significant space loss or tipping of adjacent teeth around missing tooth spaces, consider limited orthodontic treatment or space regaining before prosthetic replacement.</li><li>Add: “If financially and practically acceptable to the patient; may be omitted if patient prefers a compromised but functional solution.”</li><li>For structurally sound endodontically treated teeth</li><li>Core build‑up and <b>definitive crowns</b> placed in this phase (not Phase 1).</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_tx_plan_phase NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 tx plan phase</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 3 – Maintenance and recall</i>	<ul><li>Set recall based on combined caries and periodontal risk after Phase 2</li><li>High risk: 3‑month recalls.</li><li>Moderate risk: 6‑month recalls.</li><li>Low risk: 12‑month recalls.</li><li>Periodontal maintenance: scaling/polishing, reinforcement of oral hygiene.</li><li>Caries recall: monitor previous restorations, apply fluoride as indicated, reinforce diet counselling.</li><li>Prosthodontic maintenance</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_tx_plan_phase NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 tx plan phase</b> — list the key points for: <i>How to present this in an OSCE / SJ station</i>	<ul><li>“This 46‑year‑old patient presents with pain from root stumps in 46 and multiple carious and missing teeth, with significant financial limitations. My treatment plan will be phased as follows.”</li><li>Phase 0 – Emergency care and financial planning</li><li>Phase 1 – Disease control</li><li>Phase 2 – Rehabilitation / prosthetic rehabilitation</li><li>Phase 3 – Maintenance and recall</li><li>Say: “Because of limited finances, we will use phased treatment, prioritise disease control, and favour RPDs and limited FPDs over implants.”</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_tx_plan_phase NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm6 tx plan phase</b> — for each <i>Option</i>, give the corresponding <i>Comments for low‑budget patient</i>.	<ul><li><b>Removable partial dentures (RPD)</b> → Most affordable; suitable for multiple missing teeth; can be metal or acrylic.</li><li><b>Fixed partial dentures (bridges)</b> → More expensive than RPD, cheaper than implants; suitable where abutments are sound.</li><li><b>Implants</b> → Best longevity but highest cost; <b>generally not recommended</b> here unless finances improve.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm6_tx_plan_phase NDECC_SJ
NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b>	This is an <b>open (role-play) station</b> on the NDECC Situational Judgement (SJ) component, which assesses professional ethics, patient communication, and conflict management in real clinical scenarios. The core scenario: a 22-year-old patient was told by a colleague they would receive a crown, but today's examination reveals a <b>defective amalgam restoration</b> (open margin + distal overhang) on the tooth in question — meaning a crown cannot be placed today.	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“Agreeing to place the crown to accommodate insurance”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br>Agreeing to place the crown to accommodate insurance	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“Documenting the crown as completed before it is done (<b>insurance fraud</b>)”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br>Documenting the crown as completed before it is done (<b>insurance fraud</b>)	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“Saying anything negative or dismissive about the other dentist”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br>Saying anything negative or dismissive about the other dentist	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — list the step-by-step approach / framework (in order).	<ol><li><b>Acknowledge the patient's expectation</b> — "I understand you're here today for crown work."</li><li><b>Present objective clinical findings</b> — describe findings in plain patient language (no jargon); do not reference the colleague's judgment</li><li><b>Explain the necessary sequence</b> — restoration must be corrected <i>before</i> crowning can proceed</li><li><b>Invite questions</b> — don't rush; let the examiner feel you are thinking in the moment, not reciting a rehearsed script</li></ol>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — list the key points for: <i>Key Clinical Findings to Communicate</i>	<ul><li><b>Open margin</b> on the mesial — cannot simply be corrected; the entire restoration must be <b>replaced</b></li><li><b>Marginal overhang</b> on the distal proximal — could potentially be addressed via polishing/refinement, but in context the whole filling needs redoing</li><li>A crown placed over a defective restoration would be clinically <b>inadequate and inappropriate</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — list the key points for: <i>Managing the Colleague Comment</i>	<ul><li><b>Never</b> say the other dentist made an error or gave a wrong diagnosis</li><li><b>Never</b> defend the colleague either ("I'm sure they said the right thing")</li><li>Use this exact pivot</li><li><i>"Thank you for sharing what you understood from that appointment. I was not present, so I cannot comment on the discussion you had with Dr. X."</i></li><li><b>Immediately flip</b> → <i>"What I can speak to is today's findings: the restoration shows an open margin, which means…"</i></li><li>This protects <b>professionalism toward both patient and colleague</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — list the key points for: <i>Informed Consent Note</i>	<ul><li><i>"I'll go ahead and begin replacing the restoration today. Are you okay to proceed?"</i></li><li>That surfaces consent without turning the station into a consent lecture</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — list the key points for: <i>Rubric Checklist for Self-Assessment</i>	<ul><li>[ ] Opened by acknowledging the patient's expectation (NDEID/greeting)</li><li>[ ] Presented objective clinical findings in lay language</li><li>[ ] Explained why crown cannot proceed today (clinical rationale)</li><li>[ ] Empathized with insurance expiry concern</li><li>[ ] Maintained professional neutrality about colleague — no positive or negative comment</li><li>[ ] Declined insurance fraud clearly but compassionately</li><li>[ ] Offered realistic scheduling effort ("I'll try; no promises")</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 crown delivery amalgam overhang</b> — give details for Principle: <i><b>Veracity</b></i>	<b>What to Do:</b> Truthfully explain why crowning today is inappropriate<br><b>What NOT to Do:</b> Do not agree to crown just because of insurance	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_crown_delivery_amalgam_overhang NDECC_SJ
NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>mmm6 Darkened Anterior Restorations + Periapical Radiolucency</b>	A 54-year-old patient presents with an <b>aesthetic concern</b> about two maxillary anterior teeth: confidentist - <b>Tooth 11 (UR1):</b> Ceramic crown (done ~7–9 years ago, for aesthetics only — no prior root canal treatment) - <b>Tooth 21 (UL1):</b> Composite veneer (done at the same time, also for aesthetics) - <b>Chief complaint:</b> Darkening/discolouration of both restorations — no pain, no sensitivity, no discomfort - <b>Radiographic finding:</b> Periapical radiolucency at the apex of <b>Tooth 11</b> (the crowned tooth)	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_Darkened_Anterior_Restorations_Periapical_Radiolucency NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 Darkened Anterior Restorations + Periapical Radiolucency</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“<b>Offering only one treatment option</b> — This becomes a <i>recommendation</i>, not an <i>option</i>. The patient cannot exercise autonomy. This is listed as a <b>critical error</b> on the rubric. scribd”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br><b>Offering only one treatment option</b> — This becomes a <i>recommendation</i>, not an <i>option</i>. The patient cannot exercise autonomy. This is listed as a <b>critical error</b> on the rubric. scribd	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_Darkened_Anterior_Restorations_Periapical_Radiolucency NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 Darkened Anterior Restorations + Periapical Radiolucency</b> — list the key points for: <i>Opening (NDB ID + Greeting)</i>	<ul><li>State your NDB ID → greet patient → pick up the conversation naturally</li><li>Do <b>not</b> ask "How can I help you today?" — the appointment context is already established</li><li>Example opener: <i>"Thank you for your patience — I now have your examination findings and X-ray ready. Let's go through everything together."</i> scribd</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_Darkened_Anterior_Restorations_Periapical_Radiolucency NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 Darkened Anterior Restorations + Periapical Radiolucency</b> — list the key points for: <i>History-Taking Checkpoint</i>	<ul><li>When were the crown and veneer placed? <b>mmm6 Dabigatran POI</b> — list the key points for: <i>5. NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>mmm6 drop spatula of alginate</b>	This is an <b>IPAC-based Situational Judgement (SJ) station</b> testing the competency of <b>Practice and Information Management</b>. The scenario: dental assistant drops a spatula on the floor, picks it up, and continues mixing alginate for a patient in the chair. wisesouls  <b>Core issue:</b> Cross-contamination from a contaminated surface (floor) to the sterile clinical environment (patient's mouth), constituting a direct breach of IPAC guidelines.	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_drop_spatula_of_alginate NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 drop spatula of alginate</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1: Immediate Action (Patient in the Chair)</i>	<ul><li>Step away <b>behind the patient</b>, out of direct earshot</li><li>Calmly and quietly redirect the assistant in a <b>professional, non-confrontational tone</b></li><li><b>Do NOT</b> announce the error loudly or call attention to it in front of the patient</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_drop_spatula_of_alginate NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 drop spatula of alginate</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2: Post-Procedure — Private Discussion with Assistant</i>	<ul><li>Explain the breach: the floor is a <b>contaminated surface</b> and poses a direct risk of <b>cross-infection</b> to the patient</li><li>Reinforce the rule: <b>dropped instruments are never reused mid-procedure</b>; either leave it or call another assistant for a fresh one</li><li>Remind that gloves used near the patient's mouth <b>cannot</b> be used to open drawers or retrieve new instruments (this creates <b>reverse cross-contamination</b>)</li><li>Clarify hand hygiene protocol</li><li>Hands <b>not visibly soiled</b> → antibacterial hand rinse is acceptable</li><li>Hands <b>visibly soiled</b> → soap and running water is preferred over hand rub</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_drop_spatula_of_alginate NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 drop spatula of alginate</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3: Forward Planning</i>	<ul><li><b>Guide the assistant</b> to the office manual for review of IPAC policies</li><li><b>Remain available</b> for follow-up questions</li><li>Consider <b>team-wide IPAC training</b> (without naming the assistant) to refresh all staff on cross-contamination protocols — this reflects strong leadership and practice management</li><li><b>Document the incident</b> as per office policy; bring to the practice manager's attention if required wisesouls</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_drop_spatula_of_alginate NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 drop spatula of alginate</b> — list the key points for: <i>Scenario Variations</i>	<ul><li>A <b>bur</b> retrieved without changing gloves</li><li>A <b>hand scaler or plaster knife</b></li><li>A <b>cartridge, surgical blade, or retractor</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_drop_spatula_of_alginate NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm6 drop spatula of alginate</b> — for each <i>Element</i>, give the corresponding <i>What to Include</i>.	<ul><li><b><b>Immediate action</b></b> → Patient safety first, calm professional redirection of assistant</li><li><b><b>Corrective steps</b></b> → Discard spatula/alginate/gloves → hand hygiene → new instruments → remix</li><li><b><b>IPAC justification</b></b> → Floor = contaminated surface; cross-contamination risk; IPAC guidelines cited</li><li><b><b>Post-patient discussion</b></b> → Private, 1-on-1, educational, non-confrontational</li><li><b><b>Future plan</b></b> → Retraining, team IPAC review, documentation/incident report</li><li><b><b>Critical error (avoid)</b></b> → Firing/dismissing the assistant, ignoring the breach, not addressing it in the moment</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm6_drop_spatula_of_alginate NDECC_SJ

<b>Mock 1 cra stroke</b> — list the key points for: <i>1. Practices — Oral Hygiene</i>	<ul><li>Switch to <b>non-dominant hand</b> — acknowledge there is a learning curve; demonstrate on a model and use <b>teach-back</b></li><li>Teach <b>Modified Bass Technique</b> — still applies whether using a regular or powered brush; never scrub</li><li><b>Single-handed technique</b> (if dominant hand remains non-functional)</li><li>Lay brush on counter → same hand applies flip-top toothpaste → begin brushing</li><li>Recommend <b>flip-open toothpaste</b> (not screw-cap, which needs two hands)</li><li>Alternative: dispense toothpaste directly into mouth, then brush</li><li>Consider <b>tooth tablets</b> — dissolve in mouth, then brush</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_cra_stroke NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 cra stroke</b> — list the key points for: <i>2. Practices — Snacking Habits</i>	<ul><li><b>Acknowledge the craving</b>: <i>"I understand you're craving sweet foods — that's completely understandable."</i></li><li><b>Educate</b>: Sweet snacks/drinks have no nutritional benefit and increase cavity risk significantly</li><li><b>Gradual reduction</b>: Cannot expect overnight change — encourage reducing <b>frequency</b> first</li><li><b>Consolidate snacking with meals</b>: If they must have a donut, eat it right after lunch — not as a standalone snack</li><li><b>Healthier alternatives</b> (patient-centered): smoothies (nutritious, easy to consume), sweet crisp apples (crunchy = self-cleansing)</li><li><b>After snacking</b>: Rinse thoroughly; wait <b>20–30 minutes</b> before brushing (enamel is softened post-acid exposure)</li><li><b>Xylitol gum/lozenge</b> after snacking → neutralizes pH, stimulates saliva, reduces plaque</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_cra_stroke NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 cra stroke</b> — list the key points for: <i>3. <b>Mock 1 bite lip complain</b> — list the key points for: <i>Diagnosis — Traumatic Ulcer (Post-Anesthesia Lip Biting)</i>	<ul><li><b>Mechanism:</b> Child bites numb lip/cheek as tissue feels like rubber; child does not feel pain, so continues biting</li><li><b>Presentation:</b> Ulcer, bruising, or swelling of the lip; appears 12–24 hours post-procedure</li><li><b>Nature:</b> Self-inflicted injury — not a procedural error, not a bacterial infection</li><li><b>Differential diagnoses to consider:</b> Herpetic lesion, aphthous ulcer, allergic reaction — rule out by history (no prior similar lesions, no fever, no lymphadenopathy)</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_bite_lip_complain NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 bite lip complain</b> — list the key points for: <i>Empathy & Therapeutic Communication</i>	<ul><li>The parent's anger is <b>displaced emotion</b> — it comes from fear, guilt, and love for their child</li><li>They cannot direct anger at the child; you become the outlet</li><li>Your job: make them feel <b>seen, heard, and safe</b> before any clinical explanation</li><li>Jumping straight into clinical explanation without acknowledging the parent's emotional state</li><li>Appearing dismissive or minimizing the parent's concern</li><li>Looking at the chart while the parent is speaking — maintain eye contact</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_bite_lip_complain NDECC_SJ

<b>Mock 1 patient dismissal letter</b> — list the key points for: <i>Letter Format Rules (Critical for Exam)</i>	<ul><li>Writing in <b>third person</b> ("The patient should be informed…") — this is wrong</li><li>Using bullet points instead of proper <b>letter format</b></li><li>Making the letter sound like a <b>threat or final warning</b> ("This is your last chance…")</li><li>Writing "please consider this your <b>transfer of care</b> letter" — it is a <b>dismissal</b></li><li>Must open with <b>"Dear [Patient Name],"</b> — polite and professional</li><li>Must close with <b>"Warm regards / Best regards, Dr. , Date"</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_dismissal_letter NDECC_SJ

<b>Mock 1 patient dismissal letter</b> — explain / describe: <i>What Is a Dismissal Letter?</i>	A dismissal letter formally ends the dentist-patient relationship due to a <b>breakdown in the relationship</b>. It is distinctly different from a transfer of care letter, which occurs in a mutually agreeable, positive situation (e.g., an associate opening their own clinic and taking consenting patients with them). A dismissal letter reflects a decided, professional termination — it is <b>not a threat, warning, or final attempt</b> to bring the patient back.	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_dismissal_letter NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 patient dismissal letter</b> — explain / describe: <i>Prerequisites Before Dismissal</i>	Two conditions must be met before you can legally and ethically dismiss a patient	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_dismissal_letter NDECC_SJ

Additionally, there is an outstanding financial balance on your account that has not been addressed. Due to these ongoing concerns, I regret to inform you that I am no longer able to continue as your dental provider. > > I wish to draw your particular attention to tooth #36, for which root canal therapy was initiated but remains incomplete — the obturation has not yet been performed. This tooth is currently structurally compromised and is at significa…	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_dismissal_letter NDECC_SJ

<b>Mock 1 Incision and drain progress note</b> — list the key points for: <i>What "Fluctuant Swelling" Means</i>	<ul><li><b>Fluctuant</b> = the swelling is large, fluid-filled, and displaceable — you can physically push it from one side to the other</li><li>It is <b>beyond the alveolar bone</b>, not a small fixed gumboil</li><li>This is a <b>large abscess</b> requiring proper incision, irrigation, drain placement, and suturing</li><li>Do <b>not</b> treat it as a minor, self-limiting swelling that only needs a small cut</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_Incision_and_drain_progress_note NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 Incision and drain progress note</b> — list the key points for: <i>A — Assessment / Diagnosis</i>	<ul><li><b>Diagnosis:</b> Acute apical abscess with pulp necrosis of tooth 25</li><li>Emergency treatment options discussed with patient</li><li>Patient consented to <b>Incision and Drainage (I&D)</b> as interim/emergency management only</li><li>Note: No definitive treatment (endodontics or extraction) performed today due to patient's pregnancy — more invasive procedures deferred until appropriate</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_Incision_and_drain_progress_note NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 Incision and drain progress note</b> — list the key points for: <i>Quick Recall: Rubber Dam Clamps (for Other Stations)</i>	<ul><li><b>Clamp #13</b> → Quadrants 1 and 3</li><li><b>Clamp #12</b> → Quadrants 2 and 4</li><li><b>Butterfly clamp</b> → for the tooth being treated endodontically</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_Incision_and_drain_progress_note NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 Incision and drain progress note</b> — give details for Drug: <i><b>Amoxicillin (Cap)</b></i>	<b>Dose:</b> 500 mg<br><b>Frequency:</b> TID (every 8 hrs)<br><b>Duration:</b> 3 days minimum<br><b>Notes:</b> Pregnancy-safe antibiotic	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_Incision_and_drain_progress_note NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 Incision and drain progress note</b> — for each <i>Error</i>, give the corresponding <i>Why It's Critical</i>.	<ul><li><b>No local anaesthesia documented</b> → You would never cut open a patient without it; implies you answered a different question</li><li><b>Resorbable suture used with drain</b> → Drain will fall out; shows misunderstanding of the procedure</li><li><b>No drain, no pulpectomy, and no suture</b> → Leaves mobile tissue unaccounted for in a large fluctuant abscess</li><li><b>Incision at superiormost point</b> → Gravity will not assist drainage; incomplete procedure</li><li><b>Incomplete antibiotic prescription</b> → Missing TID, frequency, dose, or duration = critical error</li><li><b>No radiographic report</b> → Taking an X-ray without documenting findings is incomplete</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_Incision_and_drain_progress_note NDECC_SJ
NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mock 1 complicated crown fracture</b>	<b>Patient:</b> 8-year-old child, post-traumatic fall while rollerblading - <b>Teeth involved:</b> Maxillary central incisors (both) - <b>Presenting complaint:</b> Sensitivity on both teeth - <b>Clinical findings:</b> Chipped crowns (crown fractures), teeth within physiological limits of mobility - <b>Key image finding:</b> Pink blush / pinpoint bleeding = pulpal exposure → <b>Complicated Crown Fracture</b> - <b>Note:</b> If image was greyscale and pulpal exposure was undetectable, diagnosis of <b>uncomplicated crown fracture</b> was accepted and marked accordingly	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_complicated_crown_fracture NDECC_SJ

<b>Mock 1 complicated crown fracture</b> — list the key points for: <i>Option 1 – Pulp Capping</i>	<ul><li><b>Indirect Pulp Capping (IPC):</b> for uncomplicated crown fracture (dentine exposed, no pulp exposure); use calcium hydroxide or MTA, then GIC/composite</li><li><b>Direct Pulp Capping (DPC):</b> for complicated crown fracture (small, controlled pulpal exposure); apply MTA or calcium hydroxide directly over exposure; assess hemostasis within 5 minutes</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_complicated_crown_fracture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 complicated crown fracture</b> — list the key points for: <i>Option 2 – Partial Pulpotomy (Cvek Pulpotomy)</i>	<ul><li>Indicated when pulp capping is insufficient or hemostasis is not achieved within ~5 minutes</li><li>Remove only the inflamed coronal portion of the pulp; preserve the vital radicular pulp</li><li>Attempt hemostasis; if achieved → healthy pulp remains → apexogenesis continues</li><li><b>Also the preferred treatment for mature permanent teeth with pulpal exposure</b> (not pulpectomy)</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_complicated_crown_fracture NDECC_SJ

<b>Mock 1 patient complain hygiene</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1 — Welcome & Empathize with the Hygienist</i>	<ul><li>Thank the hygienist for coming to you promptly: <i>"I'm glad you came to me right away."</i></li><li>Acknowledge she has seen the review and that it targets her specifically</li><li>Empathize: <i>"I can imagine this is not easy to see about yourself. Let's work through this together."</i></li><li>Make it clear your role here is to guide and support, not blame</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_complain_hygiene NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 patient complain hygiene</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2 — Brief Reflection Check-In with Hygienist</i>	<ul><li><i>"Just to help us do better going forward — was there anything during that appointment that stood out to you, or felt challenging?"</i></li><li>This is <b>not</b> the main focus of the answer — it is a brief, caring team check-in</li><li>She may confirm all was fine, or may acknowledge it was a difficult patient — <b>either way, it does not change your action plan</b></li><li>If there is a grain of truth in the feedback, this gives a safe space to identify learning points</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_complain_hygiene NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 patient complain hygiene</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3 — Explain Why You Must Respond (Rationale)</i>	<ul><li>The review is posted on a <b>public platform</b> — it is no longer a private matter between patient and clinic</li><li>Many current and prospective patients are reading it right now</li><li><b>Silence = indifference</b> in the eyes of the public — leaving it unanswered reflects poorly on the clinic</li><li>As a healthcare professional, you have an ethical obligation to respond, but within strict professional boundaries</li><li>Responding publicly, then <b>redirecting the conversation to a private channel</b>, is the gold standard</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_complain_hygiene NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 patient complain hygiene</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 4 — What the Response Must Include (KISS Rule)</i>	<ul><li><b>Not breach patient confidentiality</b> — do not confirm or deny appointment details, treatment type, dates, or the patient's identity, even if you recognize them</li><li>Be <b>empathetic</b>, not defensive — never argue, justify, or explain the clinical procedure</li><li>Be <b>general</b> — speak about the whole team and the clinic's standards, not about the specific hygienist or incident</li><li><b>Acknowledge</b> the concern sincerely</li><li>Express <b>willingness to make it right</b></li><li><b>Redirect offline</b> — invite the patient to call or come in</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_complain_hygiene NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 patient complain hygiene</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 5 — Sample Public Response</i>	<ul><li>No patient name, no procedural details, no confirmation of anything → <b>confidentiality preserved</b></li><li><i>"Put patients at ease"</i> subtly addresses the talking complaint without making it a debate</li><li>Empathetic (<i>"sorry to hear"</i>) without admitting fault</li><li>Action-oriented (<i>"we'd like to learn more and make it better"</i>)</li><li>Redirects to private resolution</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_complain_hygiene NDECC_SJ
<b>Mock 1 patient complain hygiene</b> — for each <i>Wrong Action</i>, give the corresponding <i>Why It Fails</i>.	<ul><li><b>Ignoring the review entirely</b> → Appears indifferent; damages clinic reputation publicly</li><li><b>Deleting or flagging as spam</b> → Unethical, unprofessional; the concern may be valid</li><li><b>Defending the hygienist publicly</b> → Argumentative tone; erodes trust of all who read it</li><li><b>Confirming/denying appointment details</b> → Breaches patient confidentiality — <b>you</b> become the violator</li><li><b>Starting a back-and-forth online debate</b> → Escalates the situation; looks worse than the original review</li><li><b>Telling the hygienist to ignore it</b> → Dismissive of a legitimate business and clinical concern</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_patient_complain_hygiene NDECC_SJ

<b>Mock 1 implant refer</b> — for each <i>Error</i>, give the corresponding <i>Why It's Wrong</i>.	<ul><li><b>Taking full informed consent for implant placement</b> → You are referring out — consent for surgery is the <b>specialist's responsibility</b></li><li><b>Using four-year-old radiographs as sufficient</b> → Bone changes make them clinically outdated</li><li><b>Not flagging bone resorption risk</b> → This is the <b>critical insight</b> that separates a routine answer from a moment-aware answer</li><li><b>Presenting referral as you being incapable</b> → Frame it as <b>best-interest specialist care</b>, not avoidance</li><li><b>Calling transitional prosthesis the "final crown"</b> → Must explicitly say <b>temporary/interim/transitional</b></li><li><b>Missing any of the three referral headings</b> → All three are mandatory — reason, scope, outcome</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mock_1_implant_refer NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm5 eating disorder</b>	The prompt presents a patient with <b>anterior palatal erosion and chipped/worn incisal edges</b> — the clinical finding that points you toward an <b>acidic source from within</b> rather than dietary acid. This pattern is the hallmark of <b>bulimia nervosa</b> (regurgitation erosion on palatal surfaces of maxillary anteriors).  The station tests: - Your ability to take a <b>targeted history</b> around an eating disorder - Your <b>oral health promotion</b> knowledge specific to bulimic patients - Your understanding of the <b>systemic impact</b> of bulimia - Your ability to manage the conversation <b>non-judgmentally</b> while connecting oral and general health	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_eating_disorder NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm5 eating disorder</b> — list the key points for: <i>Interpreting the Salivary Gland Response</i>	<ul><li><b>Early/active bulimia:</b> Salivary glands are <b>overactivated</b> in response to the constant acidic environment — patient produces <i>more</i> saliva, not less</li><li><b>Longstanding bulimia:</b> Prolonged overactivation leads to fatty tissue replacement of salivary gland tissue → glands become functionally <b>redundant</b> → <b>xerostomia (dry mouth)</b> develops</li><li>At 5–6 years, this patient is more likely to still have <b>hypersalivation</b> than dry mouth</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_eating_disorder NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 eating disorder</b> — list the key points for: <i>Connecting Clinical Findings to the Diagnosis</i>	<ul><li>The erosion is <b>on the palatal surfaces of maxillary anterior teeth</b> — a site-specific pattern unique to regurgitation</li><li>The incisal edges are also involved — both erosion and chipping</li><li>This is a <b>well-established, progressive pattern</b> — it did not start recently</li><li>Exposed dentine = <b>increased caries risk</b> — erosion removes enamel, leaving dentine vulnerable to decay</li><li>This is <b>not a cosmetic concern only</b> — it is a structural and systemic health issue</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_eating_disorder NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 eating disorder</b> — list the key points for: <i>Referral — Beyond Dental Competency</i>	<ul><li>Identify the oral manifestations</li><li>Connect them to the systemic condition</li><li><b>Refer to appropriate professionals:</b></li><li><b>Physician</b> — for formal medical diagnosis (if not yet done) and medical management</li><li><b>Psychologist / Psychiatrist</b> — for cognitive behavioural therapy (CBT) and psychological support, which is the evidence-based treatment for bulimia</li><li><b>Dietitian</b> — for nutritional support and eating behaviour restructuring</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_eating_disorder NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 eating disorder</b> — list the key points for: <i>Non-Judgmental Communication — Core Requirement</i>	<ul><li>A <b>warm, supportive, non-judgmental tone</b> — the patient is living with a psychological condition, not making a careless lifestyle choice</li><li><b>No language that shames or criticises</b> the habit</li><li>Frame every recommendation as being <b>in the patient's best interest</b></li><li>Acknowledge the difficulty of habit cessation — it does not happen overnight</li><li>Position yourself as part of their <b>support team</b>, not as an authority judging their behaviour</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_eating_disorder NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 eating disorder</b> — for each <i>Question</i>, give the corresponding <i>Clinical Purpose</i>.	<ul><li><b>Do you have a formal diagnosis?</b> → Determines if they are already under professional care</li><li><b>How long have you been experiencing this?</b> → Establishes duration — patient answered ~5–6 years</li><li><b>Is the frequency increasing, decreasing, or staying the same?</b> → Assesses whether this is a transient phase or a persistent pattern</li><li><b>Do your teeth feel sensitive? When — cold, sweet, spontaneous?</b> → Gauges extent of enamel loss and dentinal exposure</li><li><b>Do you experience dry mouth?</b> → Advanced/longstanding bulimia can exhaust salivary glands over time</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_eating_disorder NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 eating disorder</b> — for each <i>System</i>, give the corresponding <i>Complication of Bulimia</i>.	<ul><li><b>Gastrointestinal</b> → Gastric rupture, oesophageal tears (Mallory-Weiss tears) from repeated forceful vomiting</li><li><b>Cardiovascular</b> → Electrolyte imbalances (low potassium from vomiting) → <b>cardiac arrhythmias</b></li><li><b>Salivary glands</b> → Parotid gland hypertrophy (early); salivary gland atrophy with xerostomia (late)</li><li><b>Nutritional</b> → Malnutrition, vitamin and mineral deficiencies despite high caloric intake</li><li><b>Systemic</b> → Potentially life-threatening outcomes if untreated</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_eating_disorder NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 eating disorder</b> — for each <i>Error</i>, give the corresponding <i>Why It Fails</i>.	<ul><li><b>Not connecting oral findings to the eating disorder</b> → Misses the entire point of the station</li><li><b>Recommending brushing immediately after vomiting</b> → The most harmful specific advice possible for this patient</li><li><b>Offering restorative treatment without stating habit cessation first</b> → Sets up for clinical failure; incomplete answer</li><li><b>Not referring for medical/psychological management</b> → Bulimia is beyond dental competency — referral is mandatory</li><li><b>Omitting systemic consequences</b> → Fails the oral-systemic connection component</li><li><b>Judgmental, dismissive, or shaming language</b> → Fails professionalism and patient-centred care</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_eating_disorder NDECC_SJ
NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b>	The scenario: <b>Patient came for extraction of upper first premolar. Upon completing the extraction, you realize you accidentally extracted the second premolar.</b>  This station tests one core principle: <b>how a professional handles an adverse event — an unintended clinical error.</b> The same backbone applies to any variation: - Root canal done on wrong tooth - Cavity preparation done on wrong tooth - Any situation where <b>Treatment A was planned but Treatment B was accidentally performed</b>  The rubric is the same regardless of which tangent the exam uses.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“Rushing into a second extraction on the same day after an adverse event is clinically imprudent”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br>Rushing into a second extraction on the same day after an adverse event is clinically imprudent	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1 — Inform the Patient Immediately</i>	<ul><li>Do not finish the procedure and hope the patient never finds out</li><li>Do not falsify records or redirect blame onto your assistant</li><li>Do not say "this is what had to be done" and normalize the error</li><li>The majority of dental lawsuits are triggered not by the error itself, but by patients feeling <b>kept in the dark</b></li><li>Immediate, transparent disclosure <b>cuts the likelihood of litigation by approximately 50%</b></li><li>Patients who feel respected and informed are far less likely to pursue legal action</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2 — Apologize Genuinely</i>	<ul><li>Do <b>not</b> apologize as a transaction: "I'm sorry, so everything else in the clinic is now free"</li><li>Do <b>not</b> deflect: "The assistant placed the instruments incorrectly"</li><li>Do <b>not</b> minimize: "These things happen sometimes"</li><li>An unapologetic delivery signals to the patient that the practitioner does not care</li><li>Patients who feel unseen emotionally are far more likely to pursue litigation</li><li>An empathetic, genuine apology reflects directly on your professionalism score</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 4 — Revisit Consent Before Proceeding</i>	<ul><li>The patient may be distressed, frustrated, or angry</li><li>They have the <b>full right to withdraw consent</b> for all remaining treatment — including the originally planned first premolar extraction</li><li>Do not assume their earlier consent for one procedure carries over after an adverse event</li><li>Pause, allow them to process, invite questions, and only proceed when fresh, informed consent is given</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 5 — Financial Responsibility</i>	<ul><li>Any treatment resulting <b>directly from your error</b> (the wrongly extracted tooth, its reimplantation, any associated treatment) is <b>covered by the clinic — the patient is not charged</b></li><li>The <b>originally planned treatment</b> (extraction of the first premolar, once rescheduled after healing) is still billable — you do not make all future treatment free</li><li>Be clear and specific — do not leave the patient assuming their entire ongoing dental care is now complimentary</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 6 — Documentation</i>	<ul><li>Record <b>exactly what happened</b>, the tooth intended for extraction, and the tooth that was extracted</li><li>Document your disclosure to the patient, the apology given, all corrective options discussed, prognosis explained, and the patient's response</li><li>Document whether consent was obtained for reimplantation and whether the originally planned treatment was deferred</li><li>Clear documentation protects you in any future audit or legal proceeding</li><li>This is <b>not</b> a mandatory self-report to RCDSO because there was <b>no intent</b> — this was a clinical error, not malpractice</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b> — for each <i>Error</i>, give the corresponding <i>Why It Fails</i>.	<ul><li><b>Not informing the patient immediately</b> → Concealment — primary driver of litigation</li><li><b>Apologizing insincerely or as a legal tactic</b> → Patients detect it; damages trust further</li><li><b>Blaming the assistant or equipment</b> → Unprofessional; you are the responsible clinician</li><li><b>Proceeding with reimplantation without fresh consent</b> → Patient autonomy violation — critical error</li><li><b>Rushing to extract the first premolar the same day</b> → Clinically imprudent; no cooling period observed</li><li><b>Making all future dental treatment free</b> → Disproportionate; only error-related treatment is covered</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm5 wrong tooth ext</b> — give the one-line / quick recall summary.	Every answer for an adverse event station must follow this fixed sequence	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_wrong_tooth_ext NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm5 pt req all ext</b> — for each <i>What the Patient Says</i>, give the corresponding <i>How to Respond</i>.	<ul><li><b>"My friend/family member got all teeth out and loves their denture"</b> → Case-to-case differences — their teeth may have had clinical indications (mobility, caries, bone loss); yours do not</li><li><b>"I only have a few teeth left and want to simplify everything"</b> → The remaining teeth are doing critical work — holding bone levels; removing them is irreversible and counterproductive</li><li><b>"I just don't want to deal with teeth anymore"</b> → Validate the feeling, then redirect to the clinical and long-term consequences</li><li><b>"My insurance is expiring and I want to use it now"</b> → Empathize — but clearly explain this does not change the treatment plan, and altering it for insurance = fraud</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_pt_req_all_ext NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm5 pt fail 2 denture</b> — list the key points for: <i>Risks — ⭐ Primary Emphasis for This Station</i>	<ul><li>Denture may feel <b>loose or poorly retentive</b> initially</li><li><b>Ulcers and pressure sores</b> are common during the adaptation phase and require follow-up</li><li><b>Speech alteration</b> — the patient must relearn to speak with the bulk of the prosthesis in the mouth</li><li><b>Dietary modification</b> is necessary — must start with soft foods and progress gradually; cannot bite a corn on the cob or an apple immediately after delivery</li><li><b>Bilateral chewing</b> must be practiced consciously to maintain denture stability</li><li>Every patient has a <b>different adaptation timeline</b> — her friend's success does not guarantee the same outcome or the same timeline for her</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm5_pt_fail_2_denture NDECC_SJ

<b>mmm10 chronic apical abscess + fur</b> — list the key points for: <i>Option 1 ✅ — Non-Surgical Root Canal Treatment + MTA Furcation Repair <i>(Most Recommended)</i></i>	<ul><li><b>Procedure:</b> Access cavity → biomechanical preparation of canals → MTA placed at the furcation → temporized for 2–3 months → confirmed healing at follow-up → definitive root canal + crown</li><li><b>Tooth is fully preserved</b> — no structural modification, no bone loss</li><li>MTA is the gold-standard material for furcation repair: biocompatible, antibacterial (pH 12.5), promotes new cementum and bone formation</li><li>Published evidence (JCDA case series with tooth #36) demonstrates <b>complete osseous healing at furcation and apex at 2-year follow-up</b></li><li>74–100% survival rate for MTA-repaired perforations in the literature</li><li>Non-surgical — no flap, no bone removal, conservative approach</li><li><b>Prognosis is guarded</b> due to the chronic, long-standing nature of the infection — furcation has been exposed to oral environment for an unknown period, and periodontal tissue is weakened</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_chronic_apical_abscess_fur NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 chronic apical abscess + fur</b> — list the key points for: <i>Option 2 — Bicuspidization <i>(If Option 1 Fails or Furcation Is Non-Repairable)</i></i>	<ul><li>Mesial half + distal half are separated, cleaned individually, restored with separate crowns</li><li>The furcation — the problem area — is eliminated entirely as it no longer exists between two separated units</li><li><b>Tooth structure and bone are preserved</b> — alveolar bone remains intact since no extraction occurs, critical given patient age</li><li>Eliminates the furcation defect as a source of ongoing infection</li><li>Maintains the patient's natural occlusion and masticatory efficiency</li><li>Good long-term outcomes reported; viable alternative to extraction for Class II–III furcation involvement</li><li><b>Surgical procedure</b> — requires flap elevation, vertical sectioning of the tooth, possible minor bone removal at the furcation</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_chronic_apical_abscess_fur NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm10 total hip replacement + antibiotics</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3 — Explain the Evidence-Based Position (Educate, Don't Lecture)</i>	<ul><li>As per the <b>COA/CDA/AMMI Canada Consensus Statement</b>, supported by RCDSO, routine antibiotic prophylaxis is <b>not recommended</b> for patients with total joint replacements</li><li>This applies equally to <b>pins, plates, screws</b>, and total joint replacements</li><li>The evidence shows dental procedures do <b>not reliably cause</b> prosthetic joint infections, and antibiotics carry their own risks (allergy, GI side effects, antimicrobial resistance)</li><li>Had the patient not shared this information, you would <b>not have placed them on ABP</b> based on the presenting history alone</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_total_hip_replacement_antibiotics NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 total hip replacement + antibiotics</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 5 — Propose Surgeon Consultation <i>(The Correct Action)</i></i>	<ul><li>This is <b>not confrontation</b> — it is interprofessional collaboration</li><li>You are not calling the surgeon wrong; you are seeking context</li><li><b>Get patient consent</b> before contacting the surgeon — you cannot reach out without it</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_total_hip_replacement_antibiotics NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 total hip replacement + antibiotics</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 6 — Defer the Prescription <i>(Safest Standard Answer)</i></i>	<ul><li>The surgeon initiated the recommendation → they retain the most comprehensive knowledge of the patient's joint health</li><li>Any post-procedure complications with the joint will be managed by the surgeon — aligning the prescription with their care is safest</li><li>You are performing a focused dental procedure; the surgeon owns the broader medical picture</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_total_hip_replacement_antibiotics NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 total hip replacement + antibiotics</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 7 — Address the Penicillin Allergy Explicitly</i>	<ul><li>Standard ABP = <b>Amoxicillin 2g orally, 1 hour before procedure</b></li><li>Penicillin allergy (rash) → <b>Amoxicillin is contraindicated</b> (cross-reactivity risk)</li><li>Alternative if ABP is ultimately required</li><li><b>Clindamycin 600mg</b> orally, 1 hour before — or per surgeon's direction</li><li>Ensure the surgeon is aware of the allergy when prescribing</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_total_hip_replacement_antibiotics NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b>	A 13-year-old patient is referred by an orthodontist for extraction of all four first premolars (teeth <b>14, 24, 34, 44</b>) to relieve crowding and improve occlusion. During your clinical exam and radiographic review, you discover that: - <b>Tooth 44</b> (lower right first premolar) is <b>congenitally missing</b> - <b>Tooth 85</b> (retained lower right primary second molar) is <b>still present in its place</b> - The orthodontist's referral slip lists <b>14, 24, 34, 44</b> — with <b>no mention</b> of the retained deciduous tooth or the missing premolar  This is a <b>clinical judgment station</b> testing your ability to pause, identify a discrepancy, and handle an interprofessional situation safely without causing irreversible harm.  > ⚠️ Critical error: Proceeding blindly with all four extractions as written on the referral slip = irreversible harm. Telling the patient to seek a second…	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2 — Take a Step Back (Do Not Proceed)</i>	<ul><li>Extraction is an <b>irreversible procedure</b> — once done, it cannot be undone</li><li>You cannot assume the referral is correct, nor can you assume it is wrong — you simply do not have enough information</li><li>There are <b>two equally plausible possibilities</b>: 1. The orthodontist was <b>unaware</b> of the congenitally missing 44 and retained 85 — the referral is an oversight 2. The orthodontist <b>intentionally</b> still wa…</li><li>Since either is possible, your <b>due diligence requires direct clarification</b> before proceeding</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3 — Communicate with the Patient (and Parent)</i>	<ul><li>Inform them that during your clinical and radiographic exam, you have identified a finding that was <b>not noted on the referral</b></li><li>Explain that one permanent tooth in the lower right area is <b>congenitally absent</b> — it never formed — and in its place, a <b>baby tooth is still present</b></li><li>Clarify that because <b>extraction is permanent and irreversible</b>, you want to make absolutely certain you are following the correct and intended treatment plan</li><li>Reassure them: <i>"This is not an emergency — by taking this extra step of confirming with the orthodontist, I am protecting your child's treatment outcome"</i></li><li>Explain that the extraction will be <b>deferred</b> until you receive guidance from the orthodontist</li><li>Invite the patient and parent to ask any questions and address them with the rationale for deferral [lecture notes]</li><li>Suggest the orthodontist made an error — keep language neutral and objective</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 4 — Contact the Orthodontist Directly</i>	<ul><li>You have seen the patient referred for extraction of 14, 24, 34, 44</li><li>On clinical exam and radiographic review, you note that tooth <b>44 is congenitally missing</b> and tooth <b>85 is retained</b></li><li>The referral slip does not address this finding</li><li>You are reaching out to <b>clarify the intended treatment plan</b> before proceeding</li><li>Option A: <i>"Yes, I was aware — still extract all four first premolars and disregard the retained 85"</i> → You may now proceed, with this confirmed verbally and in writing</li><li>Option B: <i>"I was not aware — I actually want the retained 85 extracted instead of (or in addition to) the first premolar extraction"</i> → Revised treatment plan is issued</li><li>Either way, you need <b>written or documented confirmation</b> before touching any tooth</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 5 — Patient Education (Supporting Information)</i>	<ul><li>Explain the <b>difference in size between primary and permanent teeth</b> — a retained deciduous molar is smaller and has a different root structure than the permanent premolar it is substituting</li><li>Explain why <b>occlusion matters</b> — the bite created between opposing permanent teeth on one side versus a permanent and deciduous tooth on the other side will differ, which can impact orthodontic outcomes</li><li>Explain the <b>goal of the extractions</b>: creating space to relieve crowding and allow proper alignment of remaining teeth</li><li>Reassure them that <b>congenitally missing teeth</b> are a known variation and that orthodontic treatment plans can and do account for this — this is manageable once properly planned</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 6 — Documentation</i>	<ul><li>Clinical and radiographic findings (congenitally missing 44, retained 85)</li><li>The discrepancy identified between referral instructions and clinical findings</li><li>Your decision to <b>defer extraction</b> and the rationale</li><li>The conversation held with the patient/parent</li><li>The communication initiated with the referring orthodontist</li><li>The response received from the orthodontist and the revised plan of action</li><li>Date and time of all communications</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b> — for each <i>What the Referral Says</i>, give the corresponding <i>What Clinical Exam Reveals</i>.	<ul><li><b>Extract 14, 24, 34, <b>44</b></b> → Tooth <b>44 is congenitally absent</b></li><li><b>No mention of retained primary teeth</b> → <b>Tooth 85</b> (primary second molar) is still present</li><li><b>Assumes all first premolars are present</b> → The arch is <b>not symmetric</b> — right and left sides are anatomically different</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b> — for each <i>Critical Error</i>, give the corresponding <i>Correct Approach</i>.	<ul><li><b>Proceeding with all four extractions <b>without contacting the orthodontist</b></b> → Defer extractions; contact orthodontist for clarification first</li><li><b>Telling the patient the <b>orthodontist made a mistake</b></b> → Neutral, objective language — possible oversight, not confirmed error</li><li><b>Telling the patient to <b>get a second opinion</b> from another dentist</b> → Unnecessary — the solution is simply to clarify with the referrer</li><li><b><b>Not deferring</b> and doing partial extractions without guidance</b> → No treatment until confirmed plan is in place</li><li><b>Failing to <b>explain to the patient</b> why extraction is being deferred</b> → Patient and parent must understand the rationale</li><li><b><b>Not documenting</b> the discrepancy and deferral decision</b> → Full documentation is mandatory [lecture notes]</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm10 ortho referral discrepancy</b> — for each <i>Letter</i>, give the corresponding <i>Action</i>.	<ul><li><b><b>D</b></b> → <b>Discrepancy identified</b> — referral says 44, but it's congenitally missing; 85 is present</li><li><b><b>R</b></b> → <b>Refuse to proceed</b> — defer irreversible extraction until confirmed</li><li><b><b>I</b></b> → <b>Inform</b> — explain findings to patient and parent clearly and objectively</li><li><b><b>V</b></b> → <b>Verify</b> — contact orthodontist directly for clarification and revised instructions</li><li><b><b>E</b></b> → <b>Everything documented</b> — findings, deferral, communication, outcome</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::mmm10_ortho_referral_discrepancy NDECC_SJ
NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm10 post on IG</b>	You are a general dentist in a group practice. The station asks you to <b>meet the associate and discuss your concerns</b>.  This is a <b>Written Professionalism Station</b> testing your knowledge of: - Social media guidelines for dental professionals (RCDSO) - Patient confidentiality and privacy obligations (PHIPA) - Collegial professionalism and peer-to-peer conflict resolution  > ⚠️ The station is primarily a <b>discussion and education</b> station — not a reporting or discipline station. The goal is to help the associate understand and correct the issue,…	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — list the step-by-step approach / framework (in order).	<ol><li><b>Pause</b> — Never post impulsively, especially from a place of emotion, frustration, or fatigue</li><li><b>Think</b> — Consider: Who will see this? Could it identify a patient? Could it be misread? Is the language professional?</li><li><b>Post</b> — Only post if it meets all professional, ethical, and legal standards</li><li>Avoid posting in <b>haste, anger, or frustration</b> — emotional outbursts lead to poor word choices</li><li>Any post from an account identifiable as a dentist is treated as <b>professional advertising</b> — subject to full RCDSO advertising regulations</li><li>Do not engage in <b>controversial topics</b> (e.g., geopolitical issues, divisive social issues) in a professional capacity — the impact of a dentist's public opinion is amplified by their position of…</li><li>Be mindful that <b>comments on others' posts</b> are also subject to these standards — sarcastic or dismissive remarks on another dentist's work are equally unprofessional</li><li>Maintain <b>professional boundaries</b> — avoid accepting patient follow/friend requests on personal accounts</li></ol>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2 — Tone of the Meeting</i>	<ul><li>Begin by confirming: <i>"I came across a post on your Instagram story — was that yours?"</i> → Associate confirms</li><li>Make clear you are raising this out of concern for <b>them, the patient, and the clinic</b></li><li>Do <b>not</b> mock, shame, or lecture — goal is professional development, not humiliation</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3 — Immediate Action First</i>	<ul><li>A public story is accessible to anyone on the internet</li><li>Every moment it stays up increases exposure risk</li><li>Taking it down does not undo the error but stops further damage</li><li>Instruct associate to <b>delete the post immediately</b> during or before the conversation ends</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — list the key points for: <i>Concern 2 — Disclosure of Treatment Plan</i>	<ul><li>The hashtag <code>#Veneers</code> reveals the <b>specific dental procedure</b> the patient underwent — this is <b>personal health information</b></li><li>Disclosing a patient's treatment without consent violates both <b>RCDSO professional guidelines</b> and <b>PHIPA</b></li><li>Any post from an account identifiable as a dentist's account is likely to be treated as <b>professional advertising</b> — and is subject to RCDSO advertising standards accordingly</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — list the key points for: <i>Concern 3 — Unprofessional Language</i>	<ul><li>"Nightmare," "took forever," "finally fixed it," and <code>#DifficultPatient</code> are all <b>emotionally loaded</b>, vague, and open to misinterpretation</li><li>A patient (or the public) reading this may interpret it as the <b>dentist mocking or complaining about their patient</b> — not about the complexity of the procedure</li><li>This risks damaging the <b>patient's dignity</b>, the <b>associate's professional reputation</b>, and the <b>clinic's trust</b> with current and future patients</li><li>Even if the intent was to express pride in completing a tough case, the execution fails every professional standard of language</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 7 — How to Post Correctly in the Future</i>	<ul><li>Obtain <b>written, explicit patient consent</b> — specifying</li><li>That photos will be posted publicly on social media</li><li>Whether the full face or only specific areas will be shown</li><li>The platform(s) involved</li><li>Use <b>neutral, objective language</b> — e.g., <i>"Before and after smile makeover using porcelain veneers"</i></li><li><b>No identifying information</b> — no name, no DOB, no condition, no treatment details beyond what consent covers</li><li><b>No editorializing</b> — no personal opinions on the case difficulty or the patient's personality</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 8 — Document the Conversation</i>	<ul><li>Date and nature of the concern identified</li><li>The discussion held with the associate</li><li>Confirmation that the post was removed</li><li>Recommendations made for future practice</li><li>This protects both you and the clinic if the matter escalates</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — for each <i>Problem</i>, give the corresponding <i>Why It Matters</i>.	<ul><li><b><b>Patient identity is partially visible</b></b> → Face is cropped but recognizable — potential breach of confidentiality</li><li><b><b>Treatment plan is disclosed</b></b> → <code>#Veneers</code> reveals the specific procedure the patient received</li><li><b><b>Language is unprofessional and open to misinterpretation</b></b> → "Nightmare," "took forever," <code>#DifficultPatient</code> — could be read as mocking or degrading the patient</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — for each <i>Question</i>, give the corresponding <i>Answer</i>.	<ul><li><b>Should we report to <b>RCDSO</b>?</b> → No — RCDSO is for professional conduct complaints; this is an internal correction situation</li><li><b>Should we report to <b>IPC</b>?</b> → <b>Not in this case</b> — IPC must be notified only for <b>significant breaches</b> under PHIPA's 7 categories</li><li><b>Was this a <b>significant breach</b>?</b> → No — the face was cropped, not fully shown; no name disclosed; identifiable only to clinic insiders</li><li><b>Should we <b>inform the patient</b>?</b> → <b>No</b> — the post is being taken down; the breach was not significant; informing the patient would unnecessarily complicate the situation without added benefit</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 post on IG</b> — for each <i>Critical Error</i>, give the corresponding <i>Correct Approach</i>.	<ul><li><b>Not identifying the <b>breach of confidentiality</b> as the core issue</b> → Always lead with the privacy/identity concern</li><li><b>Saying this requires <b>mandatory IPC reporting</b></b> → IPC only for <i>significant</i> breaches — this was not one</li><li><b>Saying this requires <b>RCDSO reporting</b></b> → RCDSO not applicable here — internal education is appropriate</li><li><b>Saying the <b>patient must be informed</b></b> → No — post is taken down; no significant breach; informing patient is unnecessary</li><li><b>Focusing only on <b>language</b> and missing the identity/consent issues</b> → All three problems must be addressed</li><li><b>Being <b>harsh or punitive</b> toward the associate</b> → Collegial, educational, corrective tone only</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_post_on_IG NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b>	You walk into your clinic to find your <b>receptionist and a patient in a heated argument</b> over a cancellation fee for a missed appointment. This is a <b>Written Situational Judgement station</b> testing your professionalism, conflict resolution, and leadership — not your clinical knowledge. The examiner is not looking for a single definitive ruling on the fee. They want to walk through your entire thought process.  > ⚠️ Critical error: Jumping straight to a decision ("I will make them pay" OR "I will waive the fee") <b>without walking through the process = automatic fail</b>. An answer that skips the journey and only states the destination shows poor situational judgement.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_reception_conflicts NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1 — Recognise and Assess the Situation</i>	<ul><li>Walking in to find staff and a patient in a <b>heated argument at the front desk</b> is a sensitive, unfortunate, and unanticipated situation</li><li>You are <b>bound to both parties</b> simultaneously</li><li>To the <b>patient</b>: as their dentist, you have a <b>fiduciary relationship</b> and a duty of care</li><li>To the <b>team</b>: as the clinician/owner, you have a <b>professional and operational responsibility</b> to uphold clinic policies</li><li>You <b>cannot take sides</b> — your role here is that of a <b>neutral mediator and conflict de-escalator</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_reception_conflicts NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2 — Immediate Action: De-escalate and Separate</i>	<ul><li><b>Calmly intervene</b> — do not raise your voice or match the emotional tone</li><li><b>Politely separate</b> both parties into different rooms/private areas</li><li>Acknowledge both individuals respectfully: <i>"I'd like to speak with each of you privately to understand what's happening"</i></li><li>This <b>removes the audience</b> (other patients, staff) and immediately reduces environmental tension</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_reception_conflicts NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 5 — Address Your Receptionist's Conduct</i>	<ul><li>Do not <b>fire or publicly reprimand</b> the receptionist in the moment — that is an overreaction and unprofessional in itself</li><li>Acknowledge privately that while the situation is frustrating, <b>heated arguments with patients at the front desk are not acceptable</b> regardless of provocation</li><li>This should be addressed in a <b>private, supportive conversation</b> focused on professional development</li><li>Provide or revisit <b>training on de-escalation, empathetic communication</b>, and applying cancellation policy with consistency and compassion</li><li>Remind the team: how conflict is handled reflects directly on the <b>reputation of the entire practice</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_reception_conflicts NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b> — list the key points for: <i>Improve patient communication</i>	<ul><li><b>Automated appointment reminders</b> (1 week, 1 day, and day-of) — include cancellation policy language in every reminder</li><li>Clear <b>written cancellation policy</b> included in the <b>new patient registration package</b></li><li><b>Obtain written consent</b> from all patients at intake — patient signs acknowledging the cancellation fee terms</li><li>Consider a <b>confirmation step</b> (e.g., text-back confirmation) so both sides have a record</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_reception_conflicts NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 7 — Document Everything</i>	<ul><li>Nature of the conflict and what was observed</li><li>Accounts received from both the front staff and the patient</li><li>The solution offered and the outcome agreed upon</li><li>Any commitments made (e.g., payment plan, waiver)</li><li>Date, time, and parties involved</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_reception_conflicts NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b> — for each <i>Scenario</i>, give the corresponding <i>Possible Solution</i>.	<ul><li><b>Patient had a genuine emergency + first-time offence</b> → <b>One-time full waiver</b> of fee; document clearly</li><li><b>Policy was never clearly communicated to the patient</b> → <b>Partial or full waiver</b>; update communication practices going forward</li><li><b>Both sides share responsibility</b> → <b>Partial waiver / 50-50 split</b> — clinic absorbs half, patient pays half</li><li><b>Long-standing loyal patient, isolated incident</b> → <b>One-time exception</b> as a goodwill gesture to preserve the relationship</li><li><b>Fee is valid but patient cannot pay upfront</b> → <b>Payment plan</b> — spread cancellation fee across upcoming appointments (e.g., $25/visit)</li><li><b>Patient is a repeat offender, policy was clearly communicated</b> → <b>Full fee applies</b> — but delivered with empathy, not aggression</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_reception_conflicts NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b> — for each <i>Critical Error</i>, give the corresponding <i>Correct Approach</i>.	<ul><li><b>Only stating <b>"patient must pay before receiving treatment"</b></b> → Dental care cannot be withheld based on unpaid cancellation fees [lecture notes]</li><li><b><b>Firing the receptionist</b> on the spot</b> → Private, constructive coaching — not a reactive dismissal</li><li><b>Jumping to a verdict <b>without hearing both sides</b></b> → Always gather facts from both parties first</li><li><b>Giving only <b>one rigid solution</b></b> → Present multiple solutions depending on facts gathered</li><li><b><b>Not documenting</b> the conflict and resolution</b> → Full documentation in patient file is mandatory</li><li><b>Taking the <b>patient's side over staff or vice versa</b></b> → Neutral mediator role — no sides, only solutions</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_reception_conflicts NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm10 reception conflicts</b> — for each <i>Letter</i>, give the corresponding <i>Action</i>.	<ul><li><b><b>S</b></b> → <b>Separate</b> — de-escalate, move to private rooms</li><li><b><b>H</b></b> → <b>Hear both sides</b> — staff and patient, no assumptions</li><li><b><b>I</b></b> → <b>Investigate</b> — was policy communicated? <b>Mmm10 multiple sclerosis and preventive measures</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 4 — In-Office Accommodation for MS</i>	<ul><li><b>Neck movements</b> can trigger trigeminal neuralgia flare-ups → provide a <b>soft donut-shaped neck pillow/cushion</b> to limit neck movement during appointments</li><li><b>Shorter appointments</b> to reduce physical and neurological fatigue</li><li><b>Wheelchair accessibility</b> → as MS progresses, plan for <b>safe wheelchair-to-dental-chair transfers</b></li><li><b>Avoid appointments during active MS exacerbations</b> — elective dental treatment should be deferred; emergency treatment may proceed after consulting the patient's physician</li><li><b>Positioning</b>: Keep patient as upright/comfortable as possible; avoid prolonged reclined positions</li><li><b>Drug interactions</b>: Know the patient's MS medications — immunomodulatory drugs may interact with drugs used in dentistry (local anesthetics, analgesics, antibiotics)</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm10_multiple_sclerosis_and_preventive_measures NDECC_SJ

<b>mmm3 19 year old bring 12 year</b> — explain / describe: <i>Consent and Legal Authority</i>	Since the 12-year-old is a minor and likely incapable of consenting to complex prosthodontic trauma treatment, informed consent must be obtained from a Substitute Decision Maker (SDM). Under Canadian guidelines (such as the Health Care Consent Act), because the parents are completely unavailable, the 19-year-old brother (being over 16) is legally authorized to act as the SDM and sign the consent forms. You should still document that the parents are away and ideally offer to phone them to keep them informed, but the brother has the legal right to proceed.	NDECC_SJ::mmm3_19_year_old_bring_12_year NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm3 19 year old bring 12 year</b> — explain / describe: <i>Medical and Trauma Assessment</i>	Before looking at the teeth, you must rule out severe medical emergencies. Ask the brother if the child lost consciousness, threw up, or experienced dizziness after the accident to rule out a concussion or head injury. If head trauma is suspected, he must be referred to a hospital immediately. You must also ask if the child's tetanus immunization is up-to-date, as the trauma occurred outdoors/at school and the socket was exposed.	NDECC_SJ::mmm3_19_year_old_bring_12_year NDECC_SJ
<b>mmm3 19 year old bring 12 year</b> — explain / describe: <i>Clinical and Radiographic Exam</i>	Explain that you need to take x-rays (Periapical and Occlusal) to ensure no root fragments were left behind in the bone, and to check for alveolar bone fractures.
