<b>Mmm4 hiv discrimination </b> — list the key points for: <i>Why HIV-Positive Patients Especially Need a Safe Space</i>	<ul><li>HIV affects the immune system — these patients are at higher risk for <b>oral manifestations</b> (e.g., candidiasis, hairy leukoplakia, aggressive periodontitis, Kaposi's sarcoma)</li><li>The dental team can be a <b>critical early detection resource</b> — monitoring oral cavity changes can provide insight into the patient's overall immune status</li><li>These patients face <b>significant social stigma</b> — dentistry must be a space that actively works against that stigma, not reinforces it</li><li>If they feel discriminated against in dental settings, they will</li><li>Begin <b>hiding medical history</b> from providers — including critical information</li><li><b>Avoid dental appointments</b> altogether</li><li>Eventually <b>disengage from all healthcare</b> — physician visits, specialist care, emergency care</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_hiv_discrimination NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm4 hiv discrimination </b> — list the key points for: <i>Address the Confidentiality Issue (Bonus Point — Yellow Flag)</i>	<ul><li>If you mention this: good — shows systems thinking</li><li>If you do not mention it: also acceptable — it was not the assistant's fault and is not the core issue</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_hiv_discrimination NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm4 hiv discrimination </b> — list the key points for: <i>Closing — Corrective Action (End on a Positive)</i>	<ul><li><i>"I would like you to review the office manual on our non-discrimination policy at your earliest convenience."</i></li><li><i>"I will book time with you and our practice manager to go through this training again so you are fully up to pace with our clinic's policies."</i></li><li><i>"We have an anti-discrimination protocol and training material — please go through it and come back to me if you have any questions."</i></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_hiv_discrimination NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm4 hiv discrimination </b> — list the key points for: <i>Full Answer Flow Summary</i>	<ul><li>OPEN → Confirm assumption: "I saw the HIV status in the chart after you left"</li><li>NAME IT → "This is covert discrimination based on the patient's medical status"</li><li>WHY → Non-discrimination is a core ethical principle; every patient deserves care regardless of medical status; HIV patients are legally protected</li><li>IMPACT → Safe space needed; oral health implications of HIV; hiding history; disengagement from healthcare; systemic consequences</li><li>BONUS → Flagging front-staff confidentiality protocol (secondary, optional)</li><li>CLOSE → Corrective action: retraining, office manual review, booking time with manager</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_hiv_discrimination NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm4 avulsion and consent</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1 — Clinical Reasoning Before You Speak</i>	<ul><li>Central incisor root closes by approximately <b>age 10</b></li><li>At age 11 → <b>closed apex</b> ✅</li><li>This determines: root canal treatment <b>will</b> be required (not apexification)</li><li><b>Permanent tooth only</b> → Reimplantation is indicated</li><li>Deciduous (baby) tooth avulsion → <b>Do NOT reimplant</b> (completely different protocol)</li><li>Always confirm this first — it changes your entire answer</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_avulsion_and_consent NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm4 avulsion and consent</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 5 — Prognosis Explanation</i>	<ul><li><b>Fair to good prognosis</b> overall — good timing, tooth kept moist</li><li>Most likely long-term outcome: <b>ankylosis</b></li><li>The PDL cells have likely died due to water storage</li><li>Bone will fuse directly to the root surface</li><li>The tooth may become <b>submerged</b> over time compared to adjacent teeth</li><li>It will feel different when tapped — <b>metallic sound</b>, no cushioning sensation</li><li>It may appear lower or slightly different from adjacent teeth</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_avulsion_and_consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm4 avulsion and consent</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 6 — Why Reimplant Even If Prognosis Is Guarded?</i>	<ul><li>The child is <b>too young</b> for implants (skeletal maturity not yet reached)</li><li>A fixed bridge (FPD) is <b>not recommended</b> — would require cutting down healthy adjacent teeth in a child</li><li>A removable partial denture is a temporary option only</li><li><b>Reimplantation preserves the anterior facial bone</b> — the most critical goal right now</li><li>If the tooth is simply extracted today → bone will resorb → future options become severely limited</li><li>Keeping the tooth in place — even ankylosed — <b>maintains bone volume</b> for future definitive treatment once the child reaches adulthood</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_avulsion_and_consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm4 avulsion and consent</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 7 — Future Treatment Options (Surface, Do Not Elaborate Today)</i>	<ul><li><b>Decoronation</b> — if the tooth becomes severely submerged and an implant or bridge is planned; the crown is removed, root is retained submerged under the gum to preserve bone</li><li><b>Implant</b> — best long-term option once growth is complete (typically 18+ years)</li><li><b>Resin-retained bridge (Maryland bridge)</b> — conservative fixed option in the interim</li><li><b>Removable partial denture</b> — temporary aesthetic solution during growth years</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_avulsion_and_consent NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm4 informed refusal 1</b> — list the key points for: <i>What "Informed Refusal" Actually Means</i>	<ul><li><b>Re-explained the diagnosis</b> and the reason the referral was recommended</li><li><b>Stated the rationale</b> — advanced stage of disease, limitations of in-office treatment alone</li><li><b>Explained the benefits</b> of seeing the specialist</li><li><b>Discussed the consequences</b> of not proceeding with the referral</li><li><b>Offered alternative solutions</b> — e.g., payment plans, community clinics, phased treatment to address financial barriers</li><li><b>Urged the patient to reconsider</b> their decision</li><li><b>Confirmed that, despite all of the above</b>, the patient still refused</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm4_informed_refusal_1 NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm9 discrimination of other religion</b> — list the key points for: <i>What You Must NOT Do</i>	<ul><li><b>Ignoring it</b> — giving the associate "benefit of the doubt" and waiting to see if it happens again is a critical error; you witnessed it directly and must act</li><li><b>Immediately firing the associate</b> — a single incident, no matter how serious, does not justify immediate termination; your role is to address, educate, and correct first</li><li><b>Confronting the associate publicly</b> — this happened in an open space, but your response must be private and professional</li><li><b>Dismissing it as a personal opinion</b> — dental ethics and regulatory bodies (RCDSO) are clear: refusal of care based on religion, gender, or other protected characteristics constitutes misconduct</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_discrimination_of_other_religion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 discrimination of other religion</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1: Immediately Recognise the Ethical Breach</i>	<ul><li>Every patient has an <b>equal right to access dental care</b>, regardless of religion, gender, marital status, cultural background, insurance status, or educational background</li><li>Refusing to treat based on religion is</li><li>An <b>ethical violation</b> (breach of the Code of Ethics)</li><li>A <b>regulatory breach</b> (RCDSO categorises this as professional misconduct)</li><li>A potential <b>legal liability</b> (patient could pursue legal action for discrimination)</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_discrimination_of_other_religion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 discrimination of other religion</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2: Private Approach to the Associate</i>	<ul><li><b>Clarify what you heard first</b> — confirm the overheard comment before making accusations; approach respectfully and non-confrontationally > <i>"I wanted to check in with you privately. I overheard a comment during l…</li><li>A confrontational tone will cause the associate to shut down; your goal is to <b>connect and correct</b>, not to intimidate</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_discrimination_of_other_religion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 discrimination of other religion</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3: Explain Why the Behaviour is Unacceptable</i>	<ul><li>The principle of <b>fairness</b> demands that a dentist provides care to any patient in the dental chair <b>regardless of</b> religion, gender, marital status, financial status, cultural background, educational backgroun…</li><li>This behaviour <b>violates the Code of Ethics</b>, exposes the associate to <b>regulatory consequences through RCDSO</b> (professional misconduct), and carries <b>legal risk</b> from the affected patient</li><li>As the principal dentist, it is your responsibility to ensure the associate understands this — not just to reprimand, but to <b>educate and correct course</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_discrimination_of_other_religion NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm9 principal dentist said 3 fillings</b> — for each <i>Principle</i>, give the corresponding <i>How It Applies</i>.	<ul><li><b><b>Veracity</b> (honesty)</b> → You must share your true clinical findings with the patient, even if it contradicts the plan they were booked for</li><li><b><b>Non-maleficence</b></b> → Restoring incipient lesions unnecessarily removes healthy tooth structure — that causes harm the patient does not need</li><li><b><b>Beneficence</b></b> → Preventive intervention gives the tooth the best chance of remineralization at this stage</li><li><b><b>Autonomy</b></b> → Patient must be informed of the change in plan and given the opportunity to ask questions and understand why</li><li><b><b>Professionalism / Justice</b></b> → Team dynamics must be respected; the patient should not be caught in a colleague dispute</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_principal_dentist_said_3_fillings NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm9 consent tooth whitening</b> — list the key points for: <i>Answering Patient Questions</i>	<ul><li>No. Teeth will always tend to return to their natural baseline color — bleaching fades similarly to how a bleached cloth, bleached skin, or artificial tan reverts over time</li><li>Extrinsic staining factors (coffee, wine) accelerate relapse, but even without them, bleaching is not permanent</li><li>Other causes remain: food chemicals, medications (current or future), inadequate oral hygiene, calculus buildup — there is no 100% guarantee of maintaining whiteness</li><li>No — it is an ongoing treatment plan. The initial sitting achieves a target shade; maintenance requires periodic touch-ups and modified dietary habits. Be explicit that repeat sittings involve additional cost.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_consent_tooth_whitening NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm9 consent tooth whitening</b> — for each <i>Component</i>, give the corresponding <i>Key Points to Hit</i>.	<ul><li><b><b>Nature</b></b> → Multiple appointments, peroxide chemical, protective barriers, possible light/heat activation</li><li><b><b>Benefits</b></b> → Whiter teeth, confidence boost, no irreversible tooth structure loss, professional supervision</li><li><b><b>Risks</b></b> → Sensitivity, gum irritation, burns, restoration mismatch, relapse, no guaranteed outcome</li><li><b><b>Alternatives</b></b> → Prophylaxis (tried/failed) → in-office bleaching → take-home (unsupervised risk) → microabrasion → veneers (avoid now)</li><li><b><b>No-treatment consequences</b></b> → No health risk; purely cosmetic tradeoff; teeth change color naturally over time</li><li><b><b>Cost</b></b> → Initial estimate, what's included (trays?), touch-up costs extra, NOT covered by insurance</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_consent_tooth_whitening NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm9 wrong crown from lab</b>	Upon opening the lab bag at the appointment, I have received an <b>all-ceramic crown (ACC)</b> for the patient, whereas a <b>gold crown</b> was planned, consented to, and prepared for. This is a <b>clinical error</b> — the wrong prosthesis has been delivered. The tooth preparation was designed specifically for a gold crown (less reduction required), making it <b>clinically incompatible and unsafe</b> to cement the ACC on the existing preparation, as it risks crown fracture and restoration failure. Proceeding to cement the wrong crown without disclosure would constitute <b>misconduct and malpractice</b>. The situation must be handled immediately and professionally, involving <b>two parties: the patient and the lab technician</b>.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_wrong_crown_from_lab NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 wrong crown from lab</b> — list the key points for: <i>Management: Patient</i>	<ul><li><b>Transparent disclosure</b> — Inform the patient honestly and clearly that the lab has delivered an all-ceramic crown, whereas a gold crown was planned and prepared for. Do not downplay or conceal the error.</li><li><b>Explain why this is a problem</b> — The tooth was specifically prepared with the reduction and design suited for a gold crown. Cementing an all-ceramic crown on a gold crown preparation is clinically unacceptable as i…</li><li><b>Do not offer the ACC as an alternative</b> — This is not the moment to re-consent the patient for a different material. That would be substandard practice and dismissive of the original treatment plan.</li><li><b>Genuine apology</b> — Apologise sincerely for the inconvenience and delay. Acknowledge that the patient may have taken time off work or rearranged commitments; validate their frustration or disappointment without bein…</li><li><b>Create a safe space</b> — Allow the patient to express any frustration, disappointment, or concerns and respond with empathy and professionalism.</li><li><b>Present a clear plan of action</b> — Inform the patient that the incorrect crown will be returned to the lab and a <b>rush/urgent remake</b> will be requested, aiming for return within <b>24–48 hours</b> (versus the s…</li><li><b>Book the next appointment</b> — Arrange the earliest available cementation appointment once the correct crown is confirmed to be ready.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_wrong_crown_from_lab NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 wrong crown from lab</b> — list the key points for: <i>Management: Lab Technician</i>	<ul><li><b>Contact promptly</b> — Reach out to the lab without delay, professionally and factually — no emotional outbursts, no blame-first language.</li><li><b>State the facts clearly</b> — Identify yourself, the clinic, and the patient case, then factually describe the discrepancy: a gold crown was prescribed and prepared for, but an all-ceramic crown was received.</li><li><b>Explain the clinical incompatibility</b> — Clarify that the existing tooth preparation was designed for a gold crown and that the ACC cannot be used on this preparation, making a remake necessary.</li><li><b>Request the original prescription</b> — Ask to review the lab prescription/script to determine whether there was a <b>communication error, prescription mix-up, or delivery error</b> (e.g., another clinic's crown may h…</li><li><b>Investigate all possibilities collaboratively</b> — Maintain a solution-focused, open mindset: the error may lie with the lab, with the prescription completed at the clinic, or with front-desk staff handling the deliv…</li><li><b>Request an urgent remake</b> — Ask for the correct gold crown to be fabricated and returned within <b>24–48 hours</b> given the inconvenience already caused to the patient.</li><li><b>Suggest preventive measures</b> — Propose that verification steps be introduced going forward, such as double-checking prescriptions before fabrication begins, confirming material selection on dispatch, or flagging an…</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_wrong_crown_from_lab NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 wrong crown from lab</b> — explain: <i>Key concept</i>	<b>Critical Error to Avoid:</b> Do NOT attempt to check the fit of the ACC and cement it, nor suggest to the patient "this is even better — it's tooth-coloured!" The patient has already consented to and paid for a gold crown. Revisiting consent at this stage to justify a lab error is unethical, unprofessional, and clinically unsafe.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_wrong_crown_from_lab NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm9 root fracture </b> — list the step-by-step approach / framework (in order).	<ol><li><b>Administer local anaesthesia</b></li><li><b>Digitally reposition</b> the displaced coronal segment (gentle digital pressure)</li><li><b>Radiographic confirmation</b> of repositioning — confirm fracture line is closed on the image <i>(do not skip this step — it is part of management)</i></li><li><b>Splinting:</b></li><li><b>Clearly state: No endodontic treatment to be initiated at the emergency appointment</b></li><li><b>OTC pain management</b> advised for post-operative pain</li></ol>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_root_fracture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 root fracture </b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3 — Interpreting Vitality Testing</i>	<ul><li>In trauma cases, vitality testing is done as a <b>baseline only</b></li><li>A negative result in a fresh trauma case is almost always a <b>false negative</b> or a <b>transient response</b></li><li>Vitality can be <b>restored</b> following proper management</li><li>Never recommend root canal treatment at the emergency appointment based on a negative vitality result alone — this is a <b>critical error</b> in the exam</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_root_fracture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 root fracture </b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 5 — Rationale for No Immediate Endodontics</i>	<ul><li>In the <b>majority of root fracture cases, pulpal vitality is restored</b> following repositioning and splinting</li><li>Performing root canal treatment robs the tooth of its chance to remain vital</li><li><b>No endodontic treatment should be started at the emergency visit</b> — this is an IADT-based guideline</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_root_fracture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 root fracture </b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 6 — If Endodontic Treatment Becomes Necessary Later</i>	<ul><li>Root canal treatment is <b>only performed on the coronal segment</b> (up to the fracture line)</li><li>The <b>apical segment is left untouched and vital</b> — do not instrument or treat it</li><li>This must be written explicitly in a comprehensive exam answer</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_root_fracture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 root fracture </b> — list the key points for: <i>Model Answer Framework (SJ Station)</i>	<ul><li>Local anaesthesia → digital repositioning → radiographic confirmation</li><li>Passive flexible splint × 4 weeks (2 teeth mesial, 2 teeth distal; flexible nylon wire)</li><li>No endodontic treatment initiated today</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_root_fracture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 root fracture </b> — for each <i>Fracture Location</i>, give the corresponding <i>Splinting Duration</i>.	<ul><li><b>Apical / Middle third</b> → <b>4 weeks</b>, passive flexible splint</li><li><b>Cervical (coronal) third</b> → <b>Up to 4 months</b>, passive flexible splint</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_root_fracture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm9 root fracture </b> — for each <i>Appointment</i>, give the corresponding <i>Purpose</i>.	<ul><li><b><b>4 weeks</b></b> → Splint removal <i>(do not delay — ankylosis risk)</i></li><li><b><b>6–8 weeks</b></b> → Clinical and radiographic check</li><li><b><b>6 months</b></b> → Monitor pulpal status</li><li><b><b>1 year</b></b> → Final pulpal status assessment</li><li><b><b>Every year × 5 years</b></b> → Long-term tracking</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm9_root_fracture NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm3 Internal root resortion</b>	This is an <b>NDECC SJ station</b> testing your ability to accurately diagnose a radiographic finding and present <b>multiple management options</b> with their respective benefits and risks to a patient. <b>Mmm3 Internal root resortion</b> — list the key points for: <i>The Three Rules for Presenting Treatment Options</i>	<ul><li><b>Options must be plural</b> — a minimum of two; giving only one is a critical error</li><li><b>Sequence: most to least recommended</b> — exactly as you would present options to a patient; most ideal first, least ideal last</li><li><b>Every option must include both benefits AND risks/disadvantages</b> — incomplete elaboration on either side is a critical error</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_Internal_root_resortion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 Internal root resortion</b> — list the key points for: <i>Option 1 — Non-Surgical Root Canal Treatment (NSRCT) ✅ First Line</i>	<ul><li><b>Stops the resorption process</b> — eliminates the vital pulp tissue driving clastic cell activity</li><li><b>Preserves maximum natural tooth structure</b> — minimally invasive approach; no flap, no bone involvement</li><li><b>Best long-term prognosis</b> — highest success rate when initiated early before perforation occurs</li><li>Allows complete disinfection and three-dimensional obturation of the defect</li><li><b>Difficulty in complete debridement</b> — irregular resorption cavity shape may allow residual necrotic/infected pulp tissue to remain</li><li><b>Risk of iatrogenic perforation</b> — instrumentation or obturation pressure within the irregular cavity may inadvertently perforate the root wall, particularly at the mesial or distal surface (the most common perforat…</li><li>If perforation occurs during NSRCT, additional repair (MTA) or escalation to surgical management becomes necessary</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_Internal_root_resortion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 Internal root resortion</b> — list the key points for: <i>Option 2 — Surgical Root Canal Treatment (Surgical Management) 🔶 Second Line</i>	<ul><li>The resorption defect has already <b>perforated the root wall</b></li><li>The defect extends to or involves <b>surrounding alveolar bone</b> (bony defect present)</li><li>NSRCT has failed or created a perforation requiring direct surgical access for repair</li><li>IRR is concurrent with <b>external root resorption</b> (combined lesion requiring apicoectomy)</li><li><b>Direct visualization</b> of the resorption cavity — superior access and control</li><li>Better control of moisture, bleeding, and contamination during defect repair</li><li>Can repair perforations that cannot be managed non-surgically</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_Internal_root_resortion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 Internal root resortion</b> — list the key points for: <i>Option 3 — Extraction ⚠️ Third Line (If Patient Declines to Save the Tooth)</i>	<ul><li><b>Complete elimination of the source of infection</b> — removes all necrotic tissue, infected dentin, and the risk of apical spread</li><li>No further risk of resorption progression, perforation, or fracture</li><li>Definitive resolution of the pathology in one procedure</li><li><b>Complete, irreversible loss of natural tooth structure</b> — a central incisor in the aesthetic zone</li><li><b>Alveolar bone resorption</b> post-extraction — the ridge will begin to resorb within weeks, potentially compromising future prosthetic rehabilitation</li><li><b>Aesthetic impact</b> — tooth 11 is a maxillary central incisor; visible gap with significant aesthetic, phonetic, and functional consequences</li><li><b>Additional prosthetic costs</b> — implant, bridge, or denture will be required; bone grafting may be needed if ridge resorption is significant by the time rehabilitation is planned</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_Internal_root_resortion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 Internal root resortion</b> — list the key points for: <i>Option 4 — No Treatment / Observation 🚫 Last Resort Only</i>	<ul><li>Saves immediate time, effort, and money — but this is temporary, as intervention will become inevitable and more complex later</li><li>Resorption will <b>continue to enlarge</b> without intervention</li><li>Progressive risk of <b>root perforation</b> → communication with periodontium</li><li>Risk of <b>tooth fracture</b> as dentinal walls are progressively weakened</li><li>Spread of infection to the <b>apex</b> → periapical pathology → osteitis</li><li>Can compromise <b>adjacent teeth</b> and bone if left unaddressed</li><li>What is manageable today (NSRCT) becomes more costly and complex in the future</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_Internal_root_resortion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 Internal root resortion</b> — for each <i>Parameter</i>, give the corresponding <i>Detail</i>.	<ul><li><b>Visit type</b> → Routine recall — no symptoms, no concerns</li><li><b>Patient status</b> → Medically fit, no pain, no discomfort</li><li><b>Tooth involved</b> → Tooth 11 (maxillary right central incisor)</li><li><b>Radiographic finding</b> → Mid-root radiolucency, oval/fusiform dilation of the root canal space</li><li><b>Key radiographic confirmation</b> → Radiolucency remains in the <b>same mesiodistal position</b> even after changing X-ray angulation</li><li><b>Station task</b> → State the most likely diagnosis; outline management options with benefits and risks of each</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_Internal_root_resortion NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 Internal root resortion</b> — for each <i>Differential</i>, give the corresponding <i>Why Excluded</i>.	<ul><li><b>External root resorption</b> → Lesion would be <i>outside</i> the canal outline; canal would appear displaced inward</li><li><b>Superimposed bony cyst/lesion</b> → Would <i>shift position</i> on angulation change (Clark's tube shift)</li><li><b>Cervical root resorption</b> → Located at cervical third, not mid-root; different radiographic profile</li><li><b>Root canal calcification</b> → Radiopacity, not radiolucency</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_Internal_root_resortion NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm3 pt want rct only overtreatment</b>	<b>Clinical Setup:</b> - Patient: Long-standing regular patient (5 years) - Presentation: Routine checkup - Finding: Occlusal caries (DK) on tooth <b>46</b> - Your recommendation: <b>Simple composite restoration</b> - Patient's demand: <b>Root canal treatment (RCT) on tooth 46</b>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_pt_want_rct_only_overtreatment NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm3 pt want rct only overtreatment</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1 — Open, Greet, and Identify</i>	<ul><li>Enter, greet the patient warmly</li><li>Confirm their identity (NID — Name, ID/DOB)</li><li>Establish rapport as with any regular patient</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_pt_want_rct_only_overtreatment NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 pt want rct only overtreatment</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2 — Listen First (Do NOT Jump In)</i>	<ul><li><b>Critical error</b>: Jumping straight into rationalization the moment the patient says "I don't want a filling, I want a root canal"</li><li><b>Correct approach</b>: Pause and ask <b>WHY</b> first</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_pt_want_rct_only_overtreatment NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 pt want rct only overtreatment</b> — list the key points for: <i>Addressing Concern 1 — Correcting the Misconception</i>	<ul><li>This is <b>not true</b> as a blanket statement</li><li>It may apply to <b>deep fillings</b> where the caries is in close proximity to the pulp (e.g., indirect pulp capping situations) — and even then, the majority of cases resolve and vitality is preserved</li><li>There is <b>no clinical indication</b> that a tooth requiring a routine composite restoration will inevitably require RCT</li><li>The <b>purpose of the filling</b> is to remove the caries and prevent it from progressing toward the pulp</li><li>By restoring tooth 46 now, you are <b>preventing</b> the need for RCT — you are keeping the tooth vital</li><li>Performing RCT on a vital tooth with only an occlusal cavity is <b>killing the tooth unnecessarily</b>, which is the very definition of overtreatment</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_pt_want_rct_only_overtreatment NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 pt want rct only overtreatment</b> — list the key points for: <i>Addressing Concern 2 — Insurance Is Not a Treatment Planning Factor</i>	<ul><li>The presence or absence of insurance <b>does not change the treatment plan</b></li><li>A dentist cannot justify performing a procedure that has <b>no clinical indication</b> simply because the patient has insurance coverage today</li><li>Treatment planning must always be based on <b>clinical need</b> — never on financial or insurance status</li><li>Performing RCT on a tooth with no pulpal pathology, for the sole reason of utilizing insurance, constitutes</li><li>Insurance companies conduct <b>audits</b>, and even if a dentist is not caught immediately, the fraud can be identified at any future point — the fact that others may have done it and escaped does not reduce the severity…</li><li>Dental regulatory boards receive complaints specifically about <b>prophylactic RCT</b> performed on healthy teeth — this is one of the most cited examples of malpractice</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_pt_want_rct_only_overtreatment NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 pt want rct only overtreatment</b> — list the key points for: <i>Benefits of Restoration to Reinforce</i>	<ul><li><b>Preserves tooth vitality</b> — the tooth remains alive and functional</li><li><b>Minimally invasive</b> — only the decayed tissue is removed</li><li><b>Prevents progression</b> — eliminating caries stops the path toward pulpal involvement</li><li><b>Evidence-based</b> — fully justified by clinical findings; composite restoration is the standard of care for an occlusal cavity</li><li><b>No unnecessary harm</b> — RCT is irreversible; once the pulp is removed, vitality cannot be restored</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_pt_want_rct_only_overtreatment NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 pt want rct only overtreatment</b> — give details for Scenario Type: <i>Morning session</i>	<b>Patient Situation:</b> Patient refuses Option 1, prefers Option 2 (both valid)<br><b>Dentist's Ethical Boundary:</b> Try to convince; respect autonomy if they persist	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_pt_want_rct_only_overtreatment NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 pt want rct only overtreatment</b> — for each <i>Violation</i>, give the corresponding <i>Explanation</i>.	<ul><li><b><b>Overtreatment</b></b> → Performing a procedure beyond what is clinically indicated</li><li><b><b>Professional misconduct</b></b> → Direct breach of dental practice standards</li><li><b><b>Insurance fraud</b></b> → Falsifying or misrepresenting a treatment plan to obtain insurance coverage</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_pt_want_rct_only_overtreatment NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm3 reception speak bad words</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3 — Explain the Impact</i>	<ul><li>Clearly communicate how such comments affect <b>patient trust</b></li><li>The patient may doubt the specific dentist's competence</li><li>More broadly, the patient may lose confidence in the <b>entire clinic</b></li><li>It plants a seed of doubt — "Maybe I should just change clinics altogether"</li><li>Use a relatable example: <i>"How would you feel if you walked into a healthcare provider and the front staff told you your doctor was lazy? <b>Mmm3 oral camp for elderly denture wearer</b> — list the key points for: <i>1. Denture Cleaning Instructions</i>	<ul><li>Clean dentures <b>daily</b>, preferably <b>after every meal</b> — not weekly or monthly</li><li>Remove dentures from the mouth before cleaning</li><li>Brush <b>all surfaces</b> (inner and outer) using a <b>soft-bristle toothbrush</b> or <b>washcloth</b></li><li>Use <b>non-abrasive cleaners</b> — avoid regular toothpaste (too abrasive, damages fit over time)</li><li>Best recommended cleaner: <b>liquid dishwashing soap</b></li><li>Denture cleaning solutions are also acceptable</li><li><b>Warn patients: do NOT place dentures in a dishwasher</b> — the heat distorts the fit completely</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_oral_camp_for_elderly_denture_wearer NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 oral camp for elderly denture wearer</b> — list the key points for: <i>2. Preventing Denture Falls and Breakage</i>	<ul><li>When cleaning, <b>stand over a basin filled with water</b> — if the denture slips, it falls into water and is cushioned from breakage</li><li>Store dentures at night in a <b>closed box filled with clean water</b> — not in an open cup on a bedside table (risk of being knocked over)</li><li>Avoid storing in <b>acidic solutions</b> overnight</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_oral_camp_for_elderly_denture_wearer NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 oral camp for elderly denture wearer</b> — list the key points for: <i>3. Removing Dentures at Night</i>	<ul><li><b>Remove dentures every night before sleeping</b> — non-negotiable</li><li>Clean them before storing</li><li>Store in a closed container with clean water</li><li>Gives <b>oral tissues rest</b></li><li>Prevents <b>denture stomatitis</b> and tissue infections</li><li>Promotes tissue health and maintains denture fit</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm3_oral_camp_for_elderly_denture_wearer NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm3 oral camp for elderly denture wearer</b> — list the key points for: <i>5. <b>Mmm1 rebate and promote</b> — list the key points for: <i>The Appropriate Response</i>	<ul><li>Inform them that posting discounts on social media violates professional advertising guidelines.</li><li>Explain that accepting a commission compromises your fiduciary duty to your patients.</li><li>Clarify that you will independently evaluate the appliance using evidence-based judgment.</li><li>Confirm that you may still recommend the product if it clinically benefits a specific patient, but you will do so without accepting any financial compensation.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_rebate_and_promote NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm1 rebate and promote</b> — explain / describe: <i>Social Media and Advertising</i>	Promoting coupons, freebies, or giveaways violates healthcare advertising guidelines. Patients should seek dental treatments or appliances based on actual clinical need, not because they are lured by a promotional discount. Appropriate social media use in dentistry must be reserved for patient education and showcasing cases, provided you have explicit patient consent. Sharing a discount code to encourage followers to buy a healthcare product is a direct breach of professional marketing standards.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_rebate_and_promote NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm1 rebate and promote</b> — explain / describe: <i>Conflict of Interest</i>	Accepting a 10% commission creates a primary financial conflict of interest. Dentists have a fiduciary duty to place the patient's best interests above all else, including personal income. If you receive a kickback for recommending a specific product, it can cloud your clinical judgment.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_rebate_and_promote NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm1 denture poi</b> — list the key points for: <i>Diet and Eating</i>	<ul><li>Start with softer foods like breads and slowly advance your diet over time.</li><li>Avoid tough, sticky, or hard foods like steak, whole apples, or corn on the cob for the first few weeks.</li><li>Cut food into smaller pieces outside the mouth rather than biting directly into it with the front teeth.</li><li>Chew bilaterally on both sides of the mouth at the same time to enhance denture retention and stabilize the jaw.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_denture_poi NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm1 denture poi</b> — list the key points for: <i>Oral Hygiene and Denture Care</i>	<ul><li>Remove the denture at night and thoroughly clean all internal and tooth-bearing surfaces with a brush or washcloth.</li><li>Store the denture overnight in a closed box filled with water to prevent acrylic warpage and protect it from accidental bedside drops.</li><li>Clean the oral cavity every night by massaging the gums and rinsing away any residue or microbes.</li><li>Wash the denture while standing over a basin filled with water to break its fall in case it slips from your hands.</li><li>Avoid cleaning the denture with bleaching compounds or placing it in a dishwasher.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_denture_poi NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm1 oral piercing </b> — explain / describe: <i>Oral Piercing Management</i>	Dental professionals must strongly discourage intraoral piercings due to an unacceptable level of risk for complications like hemorrhage, nerve damage, tooth fractures, and gingival recession. It is your ethical responsibility to present these clinical risks objectively and advise the patient to reconsider their decision to get the tongue pierced. If the patient insists on proceeding, you must maintain a non-judgmental approach to ensure they return for regular monitoring and subsequent oral care.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_oral_piercing NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm1 oral piercing </b> — explain / describe: <i>Harm Reduction Strategies</i>	For patients who decide to proceed with oral piercings despite professional advice, you should recommend they thoroughly evaluate the piercing artist's experience and infection control protocols. You must also provide tailored oral hygiene instructions, such as using an alcohol-free mouthrinse and monitoring the site for purulent discharge or unusual swelling. Emphasize the importance of frequent dental follow-ups so any early signs of tissue damage or material allergies can be promptly identified and managed.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_oral_piercing NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm1 oral piercing </b> — explain / describe: <i>Antibiotic Prophylaxis Guidelines</i>	Current clinical practice guidelines published by the American Dental Association and the American Academy of Orthopaedic Surgeons do not support the routine use of prophylactic antibiotics for dental procedures in patients with total joint replacements. As scaling is a bleeding procedure, you should educate the patient that blind antibiotic prophylaxis is no longer an evidence-based recommendation for their five-year-old knee replacement. However, individual patient considerations may occasionally prompt a physician to deviate from these generic guidelines based on unique medical risks.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_oral_piercing NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm1 oral piercing </b> — explain / describe: <i>Prescribing Responsibilities</i>	When navigating interprofessional collaboration, it is critical to clearly delineate the distinct roles of the dental and medical providers. The following breakdown illustrates the appropriate delegation of duties to ensure patient safety and strict adherence to clinical guidelines.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm1_oral_piercing NDECC_SJ

<b>Non verbal patient SDM</b> — list the key points for: <i>1. The Core Ethical Principle (The "Theme")</i>	<ul><li><b>Non-Verbal $\neq$ Lack of Mental Capacity.</b></li><li>A patient’s inability to speak does not mean they cannot understand, appreciate consequences, or make decisions.</li><li><b>Goal:</b> Maximize patient autonomy. You must make every reasonable effort to communicate directly with the patient before looking to a third party.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Non_verbal_patient_SDM NDECC_SJ
<b>Non verbal patient SDM</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1: Establish Communication (The "How")</i>	<ul><li><b>Direct Engagement:</b> Maintain eye contact with the patient, not the son.</li><li><b>Pace:</b> Speak slowly and clearly (the patient is non-verbal, not necessarily deaf; they can likely hear you).</li><li><b>Tools:</b></li><li>Use pen and paper.</li><li>Use a tablet/digital device for typing.</li><li>Observe non-verbal cues (nodding, thumbs up/down, eye gaze).</li><li>Watch for negative cues (confusion, blank stare, dazed look).</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Non_verbal_patient_SDM NDECC_SJ

<b>Non verbal patient SDM</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 3: The Decision Tree (If/Else)</i>	<ul><li><b>Action:</b> Proceed with the Informed Consent discussion directly with the patient.</li><li><b>Role of Son:</b> He is a support person only. He has <b>no</b> decision-making authority.</li><li><b>Process:</b> Explain risks, benefits, and alternatives using simple language and visual aids. Guardian (Court appointed) 2. Attorney for Personal Care 3. Representative appointed by Consent & Capacity Board 4. NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Replace AF filling</b>	<b>Patient Profile:</b> Has 3 sound (serviceable) amalgam restorations. - <b>The Request:</b> Wants them changed to composite (tooth-colored) fillings. - <b>The Motivation:</b> Read online that metal fillings cause Multiple Sclerosis (MS), are toxic, or are banned in other countries. - <b>Clinical Reality:</b> No breakdown, no caries. Removal is medically unnecessary and potentially harmful.	NDECC_SJ::Replace_AF_filling NDECC_SJ
<b>Replace AF filling</b> — list the key points for: <i>2. Core Ethical Principles</i>	<ul><li><b>Non-maleficence (Do No Harm):</b> Removing sound fillings releases mercury vapor unnecessarily and removes healthy tooth structure.</li><li><b>Beneficence:</b> acting in the patient's best interest (keeping a durable restoration).</li><li><b>Patient Autonomy vs. Professional Integrity:</b> You respect their wishes, but you cannot perform treatment that is not indicated or is potentially harmful based on evidence.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Replace_AF_filling NDECC_SJ
<b>Replace AF filling</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 1: Information Gathering (The "Why")</i>	<ul><li><b>Goal:</b> Build rapport and identify the source of misinformation.</li><li><b>Script:</b> <i>"I understand you want to replace these fillings. Before we discuss that, may I ask what is the specific reason behind this request?"</i> or <i>"I am curious to know what prompted this decision?"</i></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Replace_AF_filling NDECC_SJ
<b>Replace AF filling</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 2: Validate and Validate (Don't Argue)</i>	<ul><li><b>Goal:</b> Lower defenses so they will listen to your education.</li><li><b>Script:</b> <i>"I appreciate you sharing that with me. It is fantastic that you are taking an active role in researching your dental health. There is a lot of information out there, and it can be overwhelming."</i></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Replace_AF_filling NDECC_SJ
<b>Replace AF filling</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 3: Evidence-Based Education (The "Pivot")</i>	<ul><li><b>Concept:</b> The Hierarchy of Evidence (Randomized Controlled Trials > Expert Opinion/Social Media).</li><li><b>Key Fact:</b> Major health organizations (CDA, ADA, FDA) confirm amalgam is safe.</li><li><b>Script:</b> <i>"In healthcare, we follow a strict protocol called 'Critical Appraisal.' We look at the highest level of evidence—like systematic reviews and randomized control trials. Based on the most current scienti…</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Replace_AF_filling NDECC_SJ
<b>Replace AF filling</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 4: Risk Assessment (The "Refusal")</i>	<ul><li><b>Risk 1:</b> <b>Transient Mercury Release.</b> Removing the filling generates heat and vapor, exposing them to the very thing they fear (mercury) more than leaving it alone.</li><li><b>Risk 2:</b> <b>Structural Integrity.</b> Changing from amalgam to composite often requires removing more tooth structure.</li><li><b>Risk 3:</b> <b>Longevity.</b> Composite generally has a shorter lifespan and higher risk of recurrent caries compared to amalgam.</li><li><b>Script:</b> <i>"My primary goal is your safety. Paradoxically, removing these stable fillings can cause a transient release of mercury vapor during the drilling process, which exposes you to unnecessary risk. Furtherm…</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Replace_AF_filling NDECC_SJ
<b>Replace AF filling</b> — list the key points for: <i>Phase 5: Addressing the "Ban" Argument</i>	<ul><li><b>The Fact:</b> The <b>Minamata Convention</b> focuses on <b>environmental protection</b>, not patient safety.</li><li><b>Explanation:</b> It is about reducing mercury waste in the ecosystem (water/fish), not because the filling in the tooth is toxic to the human.</li><li><b>Script:</b> <i>"That is a great question. The restrictions in some countries are actually based on environmental conservation efforts to reduce mercury waste in our water systems, not because the fillings are unsafe f…</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Replace_AF_filling NDECC_SJ
<b>Replace AF filling</b> — for each <i><b>DO</b></i>, give the corresponding <i><b>DON'T</b></i>.	<ul><li><b><b>Do</b> ask "Why?" immediately.</b> → <b>Don't</b> say "No, I won't do it" in the first sentence.</li><li><b><b>Do</b> validate their research efforts.</b> → <b>Don't</b> ridicule their source (e.g., "Don't trust Facebook").</li><li><b><b>Do</b> cite the CDA/ADA/regulatory bodies.</b> → <b>Don't</b> cite personal opinion ("I think it's fine").</li><li><b><b>Do</b> focus on "Harm vs. Benefit."</b> → <b>Don't</b> cave in if they pressure you (maintain professional integrity).</li><li><b><b>Do</b> mention "Transient Vapor Release."</b> → <b>Don't</b> guarantee composite will last as long as amalgam.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Replace_AF_filling NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Aspirin post extraction scenario</b>	A 73-year-old patient presents for and completes a tooth extraction. <b>After</b> the procedure, the patient discloses they are taking <b>81 mg baby aspirin</b> daily, prescribed following a myocardial infarction two years ago. The examiner is testing your ability to identify the post-disclosure problem AND apply <b>local measures only</b>.  ---	NDECC_SJ::Aspirin_post_extraction_scenario NDECC_SJ
<b>Aspirin post extraction scenario</b> — list the step-by-step approach / framework (in order).	<ol><li><b>Summarize the scenario</b> in your own words (2–3 sentences)</li><li><b>Flag the concern</b> — history shared post-operatively; acknowledge and educate patient</li><li><b>List local hemostatic measures</b> in stepwise order</li><li><b>State observation protocol</b> — do not discharge until hemostasis confirmed</li><li><b>Post-op instructions</b> — verbal + written, include emergency contact criteria</li><li><b>Documentation</b> — note history timing, education given, measures taken, discharge confirmation</li><li><b>What you would NOT do</b> (bonus — makes the answer shine)</li></ol>	NDECC_SJ::Aspirin_post_extraction_scenario NDECC_SJ
<b>Aspirin post extraction scenario</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 1 — Recognize the Core Issues</i>	<ul><li><b>Patient communication concern:</b> Critical medical history was withheld/forgotten and only shared post-operatively</li><li><b>Clinical concern:</b> 81 mg aspirin is an antiplatelet agent that may affect hemostasis at the extraction socket</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Aspirin_post_extraction_scenario NDECC_SJ
<b>Aspirin post extraction scenario</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2 — Address the Withheld History (Patient Communication)</i>	<ul><li><b>Thank the patient</b> for sharing the information — better late than never</li><li><b>Do not blame or scold</b> — this creates distrust and may cause future withholding of information</li><li><b>Educate the patient</b> on why this information is critical: invasive procedures can cause bleeding, and knowing medications in advance allows you to adequately prepare and protect the patient</li><li><b>Reassure the patient</b> that this does not mean procedures would be refused or delayed — it means better care can be provided</li><li>Emphasize they should proactively share <b>all medications and medical history</b> with <b>any</b> healthcare provider before any procedure, not just dental visits</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Aspirin_post_extraction_scenario NDECC_SJ
<b>Aspirin post extraction scenario</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 4 — Post-Operative Instructions</i>	<ul><li><b>Bite firmly</b> on gauze for 30–45 minutes; replace if oozing restarts</li><li><b>Do not rinse, spit, or use straws</b> for the first 24 hours — protects the blood clot</li><li><b>Avoid hot liquids, vigorous activity</b>, and smoking</li><li>If bleeding restarts at home, bite on fresh gauze firmly for 30–45 minutes</li><li><b>When to seek emergency care:</b> If bleeding is uncontrolled after 1–2 hours of sustained pressure, call the clinic or go to the emergency room; provide the clinic's after-hours contact number</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Aspirin_post_extraction_scenario NDECC_SJ
<b>Aspirin post extraction scenario</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 5 — Documentation (Non-Negotiable)</i>	<ul><li>Patient disclosed 81 mg aspirin use only <b>after</b> extraction was completed</li><li>History may have been withheld or inadvertently forgotten (use neutral language — avoid accusatory wording)</li><li>Patient was <b>educated</b> on the importance of disclosing medical history and medications prior to any dental procedure</li><li>Local hemostatic measures taken (list exactly what was done)</li><li>Hemostasis was <b>confirmed before discharge</b></li><li>Detailed post-operative instructions were given verbally and in writing</li><li>Patient demonstrated understanding of when to contact the clinic or seek emergency care</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Aspirin_post_extraction_scenario NDECC_SJ
<b>Aspirin post extraction scenario</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 6 — What You Would NOT Do</i>	<ul><li><b>Would NOT</b> stop or alter the aspirin — evidence shows 81 mg/day does not require cessation for routine extractions, and the cardiovascular risk of stopping outweighs the minor bleeding risk</li><li><b>Would NOT</b> seek physician consultation for this low-dose scenario — local measures are sufficient</li><li><b>Would NOT</b> discharge the patient without visual confirmation of hemostasis</li><li><b>Would NOT</b> scold or create a confrontational interaction about the withheld history</li><li><b>Would NOT</b> administer systemic hemostatic agents for this scenario</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Aspirin_post_extraction_scenario NDECC_SJ
<b>Aspirin post extraction scenario</b> — give details for Step: <i>1</i>	<b>Measure:</b> <b>Firm pressure pack</b> (gauze)<br><b>Notes:</b> First-line; have patient bite firmly for 30–45 min	NDECC_SJ::Aspirin_post_extraction_scenario NDECC_SJ

<b>Caries risk for 11 year old</b> — list the step-by-step approach / framework (in order).	<ol><li>Restorations + SDF interim</li><li>Diet counseling + snacking alternatives</li><li>Oral prophylaxis + NaF varnish in-office</li><li>5,000 ppm toothpaste at night</li><li>0.2% NaF mouthwash weekly</li><li>Recall every 3 months + bitewing every 6 months</li><li>Optional: "The oral cavity is a snapshot of overall body health" — reinforces the importance of oral care to the parent</li></ol>	NDECC_SJ::Caries_risk_for_11_year_old NDECC_SJ
<b>Caries risk for 11 year old</b> — list the key points for: <i>Memory Aid: The "ZERO" Image</i>	<ul><li><b>Zero</b> = Xerostomia (severe dry mouth)</li><li><b>~14-year-old figure</b> = Special Healthcare Need (only ages 6–14)</li><li><b>Hospital background</b> = Chemotherapy / Radiotherapy</li><li><b>Snacks/chips in hand (not a meal)</b> = Frequent sugary food or drinks between meals</li><li><b>Magic number 3</b> = 3+ carious lesions or restorations in 36 months</li><li><b>One tooth shown</b> = 1 tooth lost due to caries in 36 months</li><li><b>Caregiver "sitting like a 6"</b> = Caregiver with caries in last <b>6</b> months (only for child aged 6–14)</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Caries_risk_for_11_year_old NDECC_SJ
<b>Caries risk for 11 year old</b> — list the key points for: <i>Step 2 — Apply to This Case</i>	<ul><li>Several cavitated ICCMS 4 & 5 lesions = exceeds the "3 or more" criterion</li><li>Frequent sugary snacks from vending machine = sugar between mealtimes criterion</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Caries_risk_for_11_year_old NDECC_SJ
<b>Caries risk for 11 year old</b> — list the key points for: <i>A. Professional Care (Biggest Problem: Active Cavitated Lesions)</i>	<ul><li>All cavitated lesions require restorations</li><li><b>Rationale:</b> Restoring today = preventing endodontic treatment tomorrow; leaving them = eventual pulp involvement → endo therapy. Restoration is still a <i>preventive</i> act — it prevents disease from escalating</li><li>If all restorations cannot be done at once (several lesions), apply SDF to arrest/control cavitated lesions in the meantime</li><li>Allows staged restoration: do 2, then 2, then 2, slowly</li><li><b>Requires parental consent</b> — must clearly inform of staining/darkening of treated teeth</li><li>Use 38% SDF on cavitated lesions</li><li>Clean the mouth thoroughly during the appointment</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Caries_risk_for_11_year_old NDECC_SJ
<b>Caries risk for 11 year old</b> — list the key points for: <i>B. Practices — Dietary & Snacking Habits</i>	<ul><li>Pack snacks from home — take away the reliance on vending machine</li><li>Reduce pocket money / allowance to limit vending machine spending</li><li>Implement a <b>"treat day" system</b> — e.g., Monday to Thursday bring from home, Friday only is treat day</li><li>Encourage the child to form healthier snacking patterns (habits at 11 are harder to change than in toddlers, but still manageable with parental guidance)</li><li>Replace sugary drinks with <b>water</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Caries_risk_for_11_year_old NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Social media commission</b>	An oral hygiene appliance manufacturing company approaches you (a dentist) to: 1. <b>Promote their newly launched product</b> on your social media, including a <b>discount coupon</b> for your followers 2. <b>Pay you 10% commission</b> on revenue from every product sold through your referral  <b>Your answer: Politely but firmly decline the entire offer.</b> Two distinct ethical violations are at play — one relating to advertising guidelines, and one relating to conflict of interest.	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — list the key points for: <i>Issue 1: Breach of Professional Advertising Guidelines</i>	<ul><li>Professional advertising guidelines explicitly prohibit promoting <b>coupons, discounts, freebies, giveaways, and competitions</b> in a healthcare setting</li><li>Patients must be encouraged to seek treatment or acquire appliances based on <b>clinical need</b>, not because a product is discounted</li><li>Appropriate use of social media for a dental professional is restricted to <b>educational content</b>, case discussion (with consent), and oral health awareness — not commercial endorsements</li><li>Permissible social media activity includes sharing oral hygiene education and clinical information; it does <b>not</b> include targeted discount offers to a follower base</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — list the key points for: <i>How to Respond to the Company</i>	<ul><li>Politely inform the company that you <b>cannot accept any percentage of revenue</b> linked to product referrals</li><li>Explain your <b>fiduciary duty</b> to patients — clinical recommendations must be made purely on professional judgment and evidence-based review, not financial incentive</li><li>Clarify that you may still independently evaluate their product on its merits; if found clinically suitable for a specific patient's needs, you may recommend it — but <b>without any commission arrangement in place</b></li><li>Inform the company that promoting their product with a discount coupon to your followers is a <b>direct breach of professional advertising guidelines</b></li><li>Your social media presence may only be used for <b>educational purposes</b>, and not for commercial product promotion with financial inducements (discounts, giveaways, etc.)</li><li>You can mention that if, after your own <b>independent evidence-based clinical review</b>, their product is found to benefit patients, you may discuss it in a clinical context with relevant patients on a <b>case-by-case…</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — list the key points for: <i>Core Exam Takeaways</i>	<ul><li><b>Fiduciary duty</b> = patient's best interest always comes first, above any financial or personal gain</li><li><b>Conflict of interest</b> = any monetary arrangement tied to product/referral recommendation that may — even potentially — compromise clinical judgment</li><li><b>Advertising guidelines</b> = no coupons, discounts, freebies, giveaways, or competitions in healthcare promotion</li><li><b>Redirection of funds</b> (to spouse, corporation, third party) does <b>not</b> remove the ethical breach</li><li>The correct response is a <b>polite but firm decline</b>, with a clear explanation of both violations</li><li>You may still use a product if clinically justified — but purely on merit, with <b>zero financial ties</b> to the manufacturer</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — give details for Scenario: <i>Patient A needs this specific appliance</i>	<b>Without Commission:</b> Recommend it on clinical merit ✅<br><b>With Commission:</b> Recommend it on clinical merit ✅ (incidental 10% gain)	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — explain / describe: <i>Issue 2: Financial Conflict of Interest</i>	Accepting a <b>10% revenue commission</b> per product referred/sold creates a direct and serious conflict of interest.	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — explain / describe: <i>What is a Conflict of Interest?</i>	A conflict of interest arises when a <b>personal financial gain</b> competes with your <b>fiduciary duty</b> to the patient. As a dentist, you are bound by a fiduciary relationship — the patient's best interest must always come first, above any personal or financial consideration.	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — list the key points for: <i>Why This Commission Creates a Problem</i>	<ul><li>When Patient B presents with a case where a <b>different appliance</b> (e.g., from another lab/supplier) would be clinically superior, the commission creates a subconscious — or overt — financial incentive to still recom…</li><li>Over time, this <b>blinds clinical judgment</b>: the dentist may stop differentiating between patient requirements and simply default to the commissioned product for every patient</li><li>The harm is not always immediate or intentional — but the ethical breach lies in the <b>possibility</b> of compromised judgment, not only in proven misconduct</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — list the key points for: <i>The Commission Cannot Be Redirected Either</i>	<ul><li>You cannot redirect the 10% to your <b>spouse</b></li><li>You cannot have it paid to your <b>dental corporation</b></li><li>You cannot channel it through any <b>third party</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ
<b>Social media commission</b> — list the key points for: <i>Fee-Splitting Is Also Prohibited</i>	<ul><li>You <b>cannot</b> tell a specialist: "I'll refer patients to you, give me 10% of what you earn"</li><li>You <b>cannot</b> tell a dental lab: "For every patient I send your way, give me a cut"</li><li>All such arrangements violate the prohibition on <b>fee-splitting and rebates</b> under dental codes of ethics</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Social_media_commission NDECC_SJ

<b>Community grant for snacks</b> — list the key points for: <i>2. Core Health Messages (General Principles)</i>	<ul><li>Frequent sugars and refined carbs → more acid attacks → higher caries risk.</li><li>Tooth‑friendly foods are</li><li>Low in added sugar, not sticky, not slowly dissolving in the mouth.</li><li>Higher in fiber or protein and require chewing (stimulate saliva).</li><li>Best beverages for teeth are plain water and plain milk; sugary and acidic drinks are harmful.</li><li>“Sipping juice or pop all afternoon is much worse than having a small dessert once with a meal.”</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Community_grant_for_snacks NDECC_SJ
<b>Community grant for snacks</b> — list the key points for: <i>5. Behaviour and Timing Advice</i>	<ul><li>Try to keep sugar‑containing foods and drinks to main mealtimes.</li><li>Avoid “grazing” or constant sipping between meals.</li><li>Encourage water after any snack to rinse the mouth.</li><li>Combine diet advice with brushing twice daily with fluoride toothpaste.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Community_grant_for_snacks NDECC_SJ
<b>Community grant for snacks</b> — list the key points for: <i>7. How to Close in the Station</i>	<ul><li>Reassure them and summarize</li><li>You will</li><li>Help them define target groups.</li><li>Promote tooth‑friendly snacks (crunchy fruits/veggies, unsweetened dairy, nuts, whole grains).</li><li>Promote tooth‑friendly beverages (mainly water, plus plain milk for those who need it).</li><li>Avoid sugary, sticky snacks and sugary/acidic drinks.</li><li>Support them in creating targeted posters, flyers, and activities.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Community_grant_for_snacks NDECC_SJ
<b>Community grant for snacks</b> — list the key points for: <i>Practice Tips for this Script</i>	<ul><li><b>Pacing:</b> Do not rush through the "Do's and Don'ts" list. <b>Community grant for snacks</b> — explain / describe: <i>3. Tooth-Friendly Snacks: What to Recommend</i>	Emphasize: crunchy, low sugar, non‑sticky, saliva‑stimulating, nutrient‑dense.	NDECC_SJ::Community_grant_for_snacks NDECC_SJ
<b>Community grant for snacks</b> — list the key points for: <i>A. Recommended snacks</i>	<ul><li>Apples, pears, berries, watermelon, etc. (emphasize whole fruit, not dried or juiced).</li><li>High water and fiber content help “self‑clean” teeth.</li><li>Carrots, celery, cucumbers, bell peppers, cherry tomatoes.</li><li>“Natural toothbrush effect”: they help disrupt plaque and stimulate saliva.</li><li>Cheese cubes/sticks, plain yogurt, plain kefir.</li><li>Provide calcium and phosphorus, help neutralize acid, form a protective environment for enamel.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Community_grant_for_snacks NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Mmm8 broken file</b>	You are performing a routine root canal treatment (RCT) on tooth #26. During instrumentation, a <b>file fractures in the apical third of the palatal root</b>.  You assess the situation and determine that <b>retrieval is not possible</b>, but you are <b>confident you can safely complete the procedure</b> by bypassing and obturating around the fragment.  This is a <b>communication and collaboration station</b>, so your response focuses on <b>ethical management, informed consent, and documentation</b>, not technical retrieval techniques.	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_broken_file NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 broken file</b> — list the key points for: <i>1. Immediate Clinical Response</i>	<ul><li><b>Stop the procedure</b> immediately once the file separates.</li><li><b>Stay calm</b> and ensure the patient remains comfortable and safe.</li><li><b>Assess the situation clinically and radiographically</b> to confirm location, visibility, and retrievability of the fragment.</li><li>Conclude that the file is <b>in the apical third and cannot be retrieved</b>, but the tooth can be <b>successfully treated</b> with appropriate cleaning, shaping, and obturation.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_broken_file NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 broken file</b> — list the key points for: <i>2. Communication with the Patient (Intra‑operative Disclosure)</i>	<ul><li><b>Pause treatment</b> and <b>inform the patient immediately</b>, before continuing.</li><li><b>Be honest, clear, and reassuring</b> — avoid technical jargon and blame.</li><li>What happened: “A small instrument used inside your tooth has broken during cleaning.”</li><li>Why it can happen: “This is a known risk of root canal treatment and is included in the pre‑treatment consent.”</li><li>What this means: “The fragment is lodged near the tip of the root and cannot be retrieved safely.”</li><li><b>Your professional assessment:</b> “I am confident that we can still complete the treatment successfully despite this.”</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_broken_file NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 broken file</b> — list the key points for: <i>3. Revisiting Informed Consent</i>	<ul><li><b>Re‑establish consent during the appointment</b> (“intra‑operative revisiting of consent”).</li><li>Ensure the patient understands</li><li>They have the <b>right to withdraw consent</b> at any time.</li><li>They can <b>choose referral</b> to a specialist instead of continuing today.</li><li>Obtain <b>explicit verbal consent</b> before proceeding.</li><li>Document this in detail in today’s progress notes.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_broken_file NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 broken file</b> — list the key points for: <i>5. Documentation</i>	<ul><li>Description of the procedural event (instrument separation).</li><li>Radiographic/clinical assessment findings.</li><li>Patient explanation (including risks and options discussed).</li><li>The patient’s questions, decisions, and revised consent.</li><li>Whether treatment was continued or referred.</li><li>Your plan for follow‑up and monitoring.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_broken_file NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 broken file</b> — list the key points for: <i>Key Exam Points</i>	<ul><li>Critical error: <b>Completing the procedure first and then informing the patient</b> — never do this.</li><li>Critical error: <b>Automatically referring</b> the patient despite being confident to complete — this shows poor clinical judgment and lack of communication.</li><li>Correct approach: <b>Stop → Inform → Reassess consent → Proceed or refer based on patient’s choice.</b></li><li>Always demonstrate <b>ethical reasoning, communication, and patient‑centered care.</b></li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_broken_file NDECC_SJ

<b>Mmm8 immediate denture</b> — list the key points for: <i>1. Introduction & Rapport Building</i>	<ul><li><b>Acknowledge the condition:</b> Explain that the upper teeth have significant (Grade 3) mobility, are "hanging by a thread," and unfortunately cannot be saved.</li><li><b>State the goal:</b> The plan is to extract these hopeless teeth and provide an "immediate denture."</li><li><b>Set the agenda:</b> Inform the patient that before proceeding, you need to discuss what the procedure involves, the risks, benefits, alternatives, and costs so they can make a fully informed decision.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_immediate_denture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 immediate denture</b> — list the key points for: <i>2. Component 1: Nature of the Treatment (Procedural Steps)</i>	<ul><li><b>Step 1 (Posterior Extractions):</b> Extract any posterior teeth first.</li><li><b>Step 2 (Healing Phase):</b> Wait 3 to 4 weeks (about a month) for the posterior gums to heal.</li><li><b>Step 3 (Impressions):</b> Take preliminary impressions (to build custom trays) followed by final impressions.</li><li><b>Step 4 (Jaw Relations):</b> Record jaw measurements (centric relation, centric occlusion, vertical dimension of occlusion/rest, speaking space).</li><li><b>Step 5 (Lab Fabrication):</b> The lab creates the denture. <b>Crucial note:</b> <i>There is no try-in appointment</i> because the anterior teeth are still in the mouth.</li><li><b>Step 6 (Extraction & Insertion Day):</b> The remaining anterior teeth are extracted, and the final immediate denture is inserted <i>right away</i> on the same day.</li><li><b>Step 7 (Follow-up):</b> A <b>mandatory</b> 24-hour follow-up appointment.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_immediate_denture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 immediate denture</b> — list the key points for: <i>3. Component 2: Material Risks (CRITICAL EXAM TRAP)</i>	<ul><li><b>The 24-Hour Rule (The Biggest Risk):</b> The patient <b>MUST NOT</b> take the denture out of their mouth for the first 24 hours.</li><li><i>Why?</i> It acts as a bandage, controls edema (swelling), and seals the surgical wounds.</li><li><i>The Consequence:</i> If they take it out (e.g., to sleep), edema sets in within 7 minutes. The denture will no longer fit, the treatment becomes a complete failure, and they will have to wait 3–4 months for natural he…</li><li><b>Unpredictable Outcomes:</b> Because there is no "try-in" phase, the final aesthetics and fit are unpredictable. The patient won't know exactly how it looks or feels until it is inserted.</li><li><b>Discomfort:</b> It is a bulky new prosthesis. It may cause gagging, discomfort, or difficulty sleeping.</li><li><b>Need for Multiple Relines:</b> After extraction, the bone will naturally resorb/shrink. The denture will become loose and require several relines (adjustments). This immediate denture is essentially an <i>interim/temp…</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_immediate_denture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 immediate denture</b> — list the key points for: <i>4. Component 3: Benefits</i>	<ul><li><b>Never Edentulous:</b> The patient will not be without teeth for even a single day.</li><li><b>Aesthetics & Function:</b> Maintains a visually appeasing facial profile, allows for continuous command over speech and chewing, and prevents the patient from "looking toothless."</li><li><b>Better Adaptation:</b> The tongue, muscles, and tissues adapt better to the final conventional denture (received months later) because they have been actively functioning with the immediate denture.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_immediate_denture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 immediate denture</b> — list the key points for: <i>6. Component 5: Consequences of No Treatment</i>	<ul><li>The teeth are no longer in a zone of recovery.</li><li>They will continue to deteriorate, risk fracturing, and cause further disease.</li><li>Current <b>FAS (Function, Aesthetics, Stability)</b> is poor, and doing nothing will lead to a poorer overall health outcome.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_immediate_denture NDECC_SJ
<b>Mmm8 immediate denture</b> — list the key points for: <i>7. Component 6: Costs (RCDSO Heavy Emphasis)</i>	<ul><li><b>Initial Estimate:</b> Give a baseline (e.g., $1,200 – $1,500). Explain that this <i>only</i> covers the initial appointments up to the delivery of the immediate denture.</li><li><b>Future Relines are EXTRA:</b> The multiple relines required as the bone shrinks are <i>not covered</i> in the initial fee. Each reline is an additional cost.</li><li><b>Final Denture is EXTRA:</b> The final conventional denture needed in 3–4 months is an entirely separate cost (e.g., an additional $2,000). The initial payment does not roll over into the final denture.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Mmm8_immediate_denture NDECC_SJ

<b>MMM8 Bitewing 2</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“<b>Cervical Burnout (False Caries):</b> Dark areas on the distal of 14 or on 44 are normal anatomy (cervical burnout), not caries. Because caries generally start just below the contact point and spread in an inverted V-shape, thes”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br><b>Cervical Burnout (False Caries):</b> Dark areas on the distal of 14 or on 44 are normal anatomy (cervical burnout), not caries. Because caries generally start just below the contact point and spread in an inverted V-shape, these anatomical shadows should not be misdiagnosed.	NDECC_SJ::MMM8_Bitewing_2 NDECC_SJ
<b>MMM8 Bitewing 2</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“<b>Mach Band Effect / Optical Illusions:</b> When evaluating margins near highly radiopaque materials (like amalgam or crowns), the high contrast against dark areas can play tricks on your eyes, making it look like an open margin ”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br><b>Mach Band Effect / Optical Illusions:</b> When evaluating margins near highly radiopaque materials (like amalgam or crowns), the high contrast against dark areas can play tricks on your eyes, making it look like an open margin or recurrent decay.	NDECC_SJ::MMM8_Bitewing_2 NDECC_SJ
<b>MMM8 Bitewing 2</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“<b>Rule of Thumb:</b> If you have to squint or debate whether a lesion exists, it is most likely nothing. It is better to take a step back and be slightly more conservative than to over-assess and document a problem that is not th”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br><b>Rule of Thumb:</b> If you have to squint or debate whether a lesion exists, it is most likely nothing. It is better to take a step back and be slightly more conservative than to over-assess and document a problem that is not there.	NDECC_SJ::MMM8_Bitewing_2 NDECC_SJ
<b>MMM8 Bitewing 2</b> — list the key points for: <i>Radiographic Notation Standards</i>	<ul><li><b>Identify the Image and Quadrants:</b> State the type of radiograph and the quadrants visible (e.g., "Bitewing, first and fourth quadrant").</li><li><b>List All Visible Teeth:</b> Do not just mention the quadrants. You must highlight all teeth present in the image (e.g., "Visualizing the canine, 14, 15, 16, 17, 44, 45, 48").</li><li><b>Example for PAs:</b> If looking at a periapical, state the central focus and adjacent teeth (e.g., "Periapical of 16, also visualizing the distal of 15 and mesial of 17").</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::MMM8_Bitewing_2 NDECC_SJ
<b>MMM8 Bitewing 2</b> — list the key points for: <i>Maxillary Findings (Quadrant 1)</i>	<ul><li><b>Tooth 16 (Primary Focus):</b></li><li><b>Defective Restoration:</b> You must state <i>why</i> it is defective (e.g., "Defective restoration due to recurrent caries"). Do not just write "recurrent caries" or "defective restoration" alone; ideal answers includ…</li><li><b>Overhangs:</b> Present on both the mesial and distal sides of the restoration.</li><li><b>Extrusion:</b> Mild extrusion of the tooth. (Missing the word "mild" is acceptable, but "extrusion" must be noted).</li><li><b>Plaque Trap / Plaque Retention:</b> Noted underneath the area of extrusion.</li><li><b>Teeth 16 & 17:</b></li><li><b>Generalized Pulpal Calcifications:</b> Present everywhere but most prominent on 16 and 17.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::MMM8_Bitewing_2 NDECC_SJ

<b>MMM8 Bitewing 2</b> — list the key points for: <i>Terminology and Formatting Rules</i>	<ul><li><b>No Acronyms:</b> The board strictly forbids abbreviations. Write out full words (e.g., "Mesial" or "Distal" instead of "M" or "D"). Write "Recurrent Caries" instead of "RC".</li><li><b>Specify Surfaces:</b> Always mention the exact surface affected (e.g., "Recurrent caries on the mesial of 16").</li><li><b>Specify Depth (Enamel vs. Dentin):</b></li><li>If a lesion is incipient, you must explicitly write "Enamel caries" or "Incipient caries."</li><li>If you only write "Caries on the mesial of ," standard radiographic notation implies the caries has reached the dentin.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::MMM8_Bitewing_2 NDECC_SJ

NDECC SJ — describe the clinical scenario / patient profile for: <b>Non-Verbal Patient &amp; Consent</b>	<b>Scenario:</b> A non-verbal patient arrives for an extraction (invasive procedure) accompanied by their son.	NDECC_SJ::Non_Verbal_Patient_Consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Non-Verbal Patient &amp; Consent</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“<b>Assuming Incompetence:</b> Automatically assuming the patient lacks mental capacity just because they cannot speak.”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br><b>Assuming Incompetence:</b> Automatically assuming the patient lacks mental capacity just because they cannot speak.	NDECC_SJ::Non_Verbal_Patient_Consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Non-Verbal Patient &amp; Consent</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“<b>Bypassing the Patient:</b> Speaking immediately to the son for consent without attempting to engage the patient.”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br><b>Bypassing the Patient:</b> Speaking immediately to the son for consent without attempting to engage the patient.	NDECC_SJ::Non_Verbal_Patient_Consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Non-Verbal Patient &amp; Consent</b> — would the following be appropriate, or a critical error?<br><br><i>“<b>Assuming the SDM:</b> If the patient <i>does</i> lack capacity, blindly assuming the son is the correct Substitute Decision Maker (SDM) without verifying the legal hierarchy.”</i>	<b>Critical error / Avoid this.</b><br><br><b>Assuming the SDM:</b> If the patient <i>does</i> lack capacity, blindly assuming the son is the correct Substitute Decision Maker (SDM) without verifying the legal hierarchy.	NDECC_SJ::Non_Verbal_Patient_Consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Non-Verbal Patient &amp; Consent</b> — list the key points for: <i>1. The Core Ethical Principle (The "Theme")</i>	<ul><li><b>Non-Verbal $\neq$ Lack of Mental Capacity.</b></li><li>A patient’s inability to speak does not mean they cannot understand, appreciate consequences, or make decisions.</li><li><b>Goal:</b> Maximize patient autonomy. You must make every reasonable effort to communicate directly with the patient before looking to a third party.</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Non_Verbal_Patient_Consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Non-Verbal Patient &amp; Consent</b> — list the key points for: <i>4. Documentation (Record Keeping)</i>	<ul><li><i>If capable:</i> "Patient demonstrated understanding via and provided consent."</li><li><i>If incapable:</i> "Patient was unable to appreciate consequences due to [observation, e.g., lack of response/confusion]. Deemed to lack capacity for this specific treatment."</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Non_Verbal_Patient_Consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Non-Verbal Patient &amp; Consent</b> — list the key points for: <i>5. Key Rationale Phrases for Your Answer</i>	<ul><li>"My professional obligation is to facilitate communication and not rob the patient of their autonomy."</li><li>"I will not conflate a communication barrier with a cognitive deficit."</li><li>"Informed consent is a process, not just a signature. I must ensure the patient comprehends the risks of this invasive procedure."</li><li>"I must adhere to the HCCA (Health Care Consent Act) hierarchy to identify the correct Substitute Decision Maker."</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Non_Verbal_Patient_Consent NDECC_SJ
<b>Non-Verbal Patient &amp; Consent</b> — list the key points for: <i>Summary Checklist</i>	<ul><li>[ ] Did I talk to the patient first?</li><li>[ ] Did I use visual aids/writing tools?</li><li>[ ] Did I assess capacity separately from speech?</li><li>[ ] Did I check the hierarchy before letting the son sign?</li><li>[ ] Is it all written in the chart?</li></ul>	NDECC_SJ::Non_Verbal_Patient_Consent NDECC_SJ
